Einstein vacuum equations with 𝕌 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbb{U}(1) symmetry in an elliptic gauge: local well-posedness and blow-up criterium
Arthur Touati

Abstract
. In this article, we are interested in the Einstein vacuum equations on a Lorentzian manifold displaying 𝕌 ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathbb{U}(1) symmetry. We identify some freely prescribable initial data, solve the constraint equations and prove the existence of a unique and local in time solution at the H 3 H^{3} level. In addition, we prove a blow-up criterium at the H 2 H^{2} level. By doing so, we improve a result of Huneau and Luk in [ 8 ] on a similar system, and our main motivation is to provide a framework adapted to the study of high-frequency solutions to the Einstein vacuum equations done in a forthcoming paper by Huneau and Luk. As a consequence we work in an elliptic gauge, particularly adapted to the handling of high-frequency solutions, which have large high-order norms.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Presentation of the results

In this article, we are interesting in solving the Einstein vacuum equations

R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=0

on a four-dimensional lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},^{(4)}g)
, where
R_{\mu\nu}
and
R
are the Ricci tensor and the scalar curvature associated to
{}^{(4)}g
. We assume that the manifold
\mathcal{M}
amits a translation Killing field, this symmetry being called the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry. Thanks to this symmetry, the
3+1
Einstein vacuum equations reduce to the
2+1
Einstein equations coupled with two scalar fields satisfying a wave map system:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Box_{g}\varphi=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\omega\\
\Box_{g}\omega=4\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\varphi\\
R_{\mu\nu}(g)=2\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{\mu}\omega\partial_{\nu}\omega\end{array}\right.

where
\varphi
and
\omega
are the two scalar fields and
g
is a
2+1
lorentzian metric appearing in the decomposition of
{}^{(4)}g
(see Section
2.2
for more details).

The goal of this paper is to solve the previous system in an elliptic gauge. This particular choice of gauge for the
2+1
spacetime will be precisely defined in Section
2.3
, but let us just say for now that it allows us to recast the Einstein equations as a system of
semilinear elliptic equations
for the metric coefficients. This gauge is therefore especially useful for low-regularity problems, since it offers additional regularity for the metric.

More precisely, we obtain two results on this system: local well-posedness with some precise smallness assumptions and a blow-up criterium. Both these results can be roughly stated as follows (see Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
for some precise statements):

Theorem 1.1

(Rough version of Theorem
3.1
)
.

Given admissible initial data for
(\varphi,\omega)
large in
H^{3}
and small enough in
W^{1,4}
(the smallness threshold being independent of the potentially large
H^{3}
-norms), there exists a unique solution to (
1.1
) in the elliptic gauge on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
for some
T>0
depending on the initial
H^{3}
-norms.

Theorem 1.2

(Rough version of Theorem
3.2
)
.

If the time of existence
T
of the solution obtained in Theorem
1.1
is finite, then the
H^{2}
norm of
(\varphi,\omega)
diverges at
T
or the smallness in
W^{1,4}
no longer holds.

1.2.
Strategy of proof and main challenges

Let us briefly discuss the strategy employed to prove the two previous theorems and point out the main challenges we face. We adopt the same global strategy as in the work of Huneau and Luk
[
8
]
, and we will discuss the differences and similarities with this article in Section
1.3.1
.

1.2.1.
Theorem
1.1

In order to prove Theorem
1.1
, we need to solve the Einstein equations in the elliptic gauge. As the name of the gauge suggests, the system (
1.1
) then reads

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Box_{g}U=(\partial U)^{2}\\
\Delta g=(\partial U)^{2}+(\partial g)^{2}\end{array}\right.

where
U
denotes either
\varphi
or
\omega
and in the second equation
g
denotes any metric coefficient. One of the main challenges of solving such a system is therefore the inversion of the Laplacian operator on a unbounded set, here
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Indeed this will imply that some of the metric coefficients, the lapse
N
and the conformal factor
\gamma
(see Section
2.3
for their definitions), presents some logarithmic growth at spacelike infinity. To counteract these growth, we work in the whole paper with weighted Sobolev spaces (see Definition
2.1
).

One major aspect of Theorem
1.1
is that the smallness assumed for the initial data is only at the
W^{1,4}
level, while their higher order norms can be arbitrarily large. It is quite unusual to require some smallness on the initial data to only prove
local
existence, usually one would only ask for smallness on the time of existence. Here however, the smallness of the time of existence can only be of help when performing energy estimates for the hyperbolic part of (
1.2
). When dealing with the non-linearities in the elliptic part of (
1.2
), we rely on the smallness of the solution to close the hierarchy of estimates we introduce.

However, one of the strength of our result is that the smallness of the initial data is only assumed for their first derivatives in
L^{4}
topology. The higher order norm, i.e the
L^{2}
norm of their second and third derivatives can be large, and this largeness is
not
compensated by the smallness ot the initial data, which concretely means that the smallness threshold in Theorem
1.1
doesn’t depend on the
H^{3}
norm of the initial data. This initial data regime (largeness in
H^{3}
not compensated by smallness in
W^{1,4}
) is motivated by the main application of this article, namely to the construction of high-frequency spacetimes in the context of the Burnett conjecture in general relativity. See Huneau and Luk’s article
[
7
]
for the application of the present article and
[
1
]
for the original paper of Burnett.

Despite the particularities of the elliptic gauge we just discussed, the global strategy to solve the Einstein vacuum equations is standard:

•

we first solve the
constraint equations
and by doing so construct initial data for the metric on the slice
\{t=0\}
which in particular satisfies the gauge conditions,

•

then, we solve a
reduced system
, which in our case is a coupled system of elliptic, wave and transport equations,

•

finally, we prove using the Bianchi identity that solving the reduced system actually implies the full Einstein vacuum equations and the propagation of the gauge conditions.

As a final comment, note that wave map structure of the hyperbolic part of (
1.2
) plays no role in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

1.2.2.
Theorem
1.2

Inversely, the wave map structure of the coupling between the wave equations for
\varphi
and
\omega
is at the heart of Theorem’s
1.1
proof. This result basically means that the
H^{2}
norm of the initial data controls the time of existence of the solution (as long as the smallness in
W^{1,4}
holds), whereas we need
H^{3}
regularity to prove local existence. This is not a consequence of the standard energy method for the wave equation, since in dimension 2 it only allows for
H^{2+\varepsilon}
regularity. Reaching
H^{2}
requires therefore to use another structure, in our case the wave map structure of the hyperbolic part of (
1.2
). Since the work of Choquet-Bruhat in
[
2
]
it is well-known that we can associate to any wave map systems a
third order energy estimate
, which we crucially use to reach
H^{2}
.

As explain above, we rely on the smallness of the initial data to prove local-existence. This requirement has the following consequence: we are unable to prove local well-posedness at the
H^{2}
level. Indeed, in order to obtain such a result, we would need (in addition to the third order energy estimate) to propagate the smallness in
W^{1,4}
through the wave map system using only
H^{2}
norms. This is not possible in dimension 2 using only energy estimates. Therefore, we need to assume that the
W^{1,4}
smallness is propagated, which explains why we ”only” prove a blow-up criterium and not local well-posedness at the
H^{2}
level.

1.3.
Relation to previous works

In this section we discuss the link of our work with the litterature. To say it briefly, the proof of Theorem
1.1
draws from
[
8
]
and the proof of Theorem
1.2
uses tehniques from Choquet-Bruhat.

1.3.1.
An improvement of
[
8
]

This work has a lot of common points with the work of Huneau and Luk in
[
8
]
, where they also study the system (
1.1
). In this section, let us detail the similarities and differences between these two works.

The system actually solved in
[
8
]
is the Einstein
null dust
system in
polarized

\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry. The polarized assumption implies
\omega=0
, and thus simplifies the hyperbolic part of the Einstein equations: a classical linear wave equation replaces our wave map system and its non-linear coupling associated to the non-polarized case we study here.

The Einstein null dust system is a particular case of Einstein Vlasov system and is translated as follows: the system studied in
[
8
]
is coupled with some transport equations for massless particles along null geodesics. This involves the solving of the eikonal equation and thus requires the use of the null structure in
2+1
dimension to avoid a loss of derivatives. Since we solve the Einstein
vacuum
equations, this difficulty disappears in our work.

As explained earlier in this introduction, the actual structure of the hyperbolic part of (
1.1
) doesn’t influence the proof of the local existence of solutions. The proof given here nevertheless differs from the one of
[
8
]
because of the differences in terms of regularity of the initial data. In
[
8
]
, the initial data enjoy
H^{4}
regularity and are small in
W^{1,\infty}
. This should be compared to our assumptions:
H^{3}
regularity with smallness in
W^{1,4}
only. Because of this fact, the hierarchy of estimates we introduce during the bootstrap argument differs from the one introduced in
[
8
]
.

1.3.2.
Symmetry and wave maps

As explained in the seventh chapter of the appendix of
[
3
]
, the presence of a symmetry group acting on the spacetime generically implies the reduction of the Einstein vacuum equations into a coupled system between some Einstein-type equations and a wave map system. This is in particular the case for the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry. In
[
10
]
, Moncrief performed this reduction and in
[
4
]
Choquet-Bruhat and Moncrief prove local-existence at the
H^{2}
level for a manifold of the form
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\Sigma\times\mathbb{U}(1)
where
\Sigma
is a compact two-dimensional manifold. The compactness of
\Sigma
allows them to use Schauder fixed point theorem, thus avoiding the need for some initial smallness. This has to be compared to the present work, where we need some initial smallness to solve the PDE system.

As explained earlier in this introduction, the wave map structure is particularly important for the proof of Theorem
1.2
. Indeed, as noted by Choquet-Bruhat in
[
2
]
in the most general case, it is always possible to associate to any wave map system a third order energy estimate (see also
[
11
]
).

2.
Geometrical setting

In this section, we first introduce our notations, and then we present the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry and the elliptic gauge.

2.1.
Notations

In this section we introduce the notations of this article. We will be working on
\mathcal{M}\vcentcolon=I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval. This space will be given a coordinate system
(t,x^{1},x^{2})
. We will use
x^{i}
with lower case Latin index
i=1,2
to denote the spatial coordinates.

Convention with indices :

•

Lower case Latin indices run through the spatial indices 1, 2, while lower case Greek indices run through all the spacetime indices. Moreover, repeat indices are always summed over their natural range.

•

Lower case Latin indices are always raised and lowered with respect to the standard Euclidean metric
\delta_{ij}
, while lower case Greek indices are raised and lowered with respect to the spacetime metric
g
.

Differential operators :

•

For a function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2+1}
, we set
\partial f=(\partial_{t}f,\nabla f)
, where
\nabla f
is the usual spatial gradient on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Samewise,
\Delta
denotes the standard Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. If
A=(a_{1},a_{2})
and
B=(b_{1},b_{2})
are two vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we use the dot notation for their scalar product

A\cdot B=a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}=\delta^{ij}a_{i}b_{j}.

The notation
|\cdot|
is reserved for the norm associated to this scalar product, meaning
|A|^{2}=A\cdot A
.

•

\mathcal{L}
denotes the Lie derivatives,
D
denotes the Levi-Civita connection associated to the spacetime metric
g
, and
\Box_{g}
denotes the d’Alembertian operator on functions :

\Box_{g}f=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det(g)|}}\partial_{\mu}\left(\left(g^{-1}\right)^{\mu\nu}\sqrt{|\det(g)|}\partial_{\nu}f\right).

•

L
denotes the euclidean conformal Killing operator acting on vectors on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to give a symmetric traceless (with respect to
\delta
) covariant 2-tensor :

(L\xi)_{ij}\vcentcolon=\delta_{j\ell}\partial_{i}\xi^{\ell}+\delta_{i\ell}\partial_{j}\xi^{\ell}-\delta_{ij}\partial_{k}\xi^{k}.

Functions spaces :

We will work with standard functions spaces
L^{p}
,
H^{k}
,
C^{m}
,
C^{\infty}_{c}
, etc., and assume their standard definitions. We use the following convention :

•

All function spaces will be taken on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the measures will be the 2D-Lebesgue measure.

•

When applied to quantities defined on a spacetime
I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the norms
L^{p}
,
H^{k}
,
C^{m}
denote fixed-time norms. In particular, if in an estimate the time
t\in I
in question is not explicitly stated, then it means that the estimate holds for all
t\in I
for the time interval
I
that is appropriate for the context.

We will also work in weighted Sobolev spaces, which are well-suited to elliptic equations. We recall here their definition, together with the definition of weighted Hölder space. The properties of these spaces that
we need are listed in Appendix
B
. We use the standard notation
\langle x\rangle=\left(1+|x|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<p<\infty
,
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
. The weighted Sobolev space
W^{m,p}_{\delta}
is the completion of
C^{\infty}_{0}
under the norm

\|u\|_{W^{m,p}_{\delta}}=\sum_{|\beta|\leq m}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta+\beta}\nabla^{\beta}u\right\|_{L^{p}}.

We will use the notation
H^{m}_{\delta}=W^{m,2}_{\delta}
,
L^{p}_{\delta}=W^{0,p}_{\delta}
and
W^{m,p}
denotes the standard Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The weighted Hölder space
C^{m}_{\delta}
is the completion of
C^{m}_{c}
under the norm

\|u\|_{C^{m}_{\delta}}=\sum_{|\beta|\leq m}\left\|\langle x\rangle^{\delta+\beta}\nabla^{\beta}u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

For a covariant 2-tensor
A_{ij}
tangential to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we use the convention :

\|A\|_{X}=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\|A_{ij}\|_{X},

where
X
stands for any function spaces defined above.

We denote by
B_{r}
the ball in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of radius
r
centered at 0.

2.2.
Einstein vacuum equations with a translation Killing field

In this section, we present the
\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry. From now on, we consider a Lorentzian manifold
(I\times\mathbb{R}^{3},^{(4)}g)
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval, and
{}^{(4)}g
is a Lorentzian metric, for which
\partial_{3}
is a Killing field. Following the Appendix VII of
[
3
]
, this is equivalent to say that
{}^{(4)}g
has the following form :

(2.1)

{}^{(4)}g=e^{-2\varphi}g+e^{2\varphi}(\mathrm{d}x^{3}+A_{\alpha}\mathrm{d}x^{\alpha})^{2},

where
\varphi:I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a scalar function,
g
is a Lorentzian metric on
I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
A
is a 1-form on
I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The
polarized

\mathbb{U}(1)
symmetry is the case where
A=0
. We extend
\varphi
to a function on
I\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
in such a way that
\varphi
does not depend on
x^{3}
. Given this ansatz of the metric, the vector field
\partial_{3}
is Killing and hypersurface orthogonal. Assuming that the metric
{}^{(4)}g
satisfies the Einstein vacuum equations, i.e
R_{\mu\nu}(^{(4)}g)=0
, one can prove that there exists a function
\omega
such that

F=-e^{-3\varphi}*\mathrm{d}\omega

where
F_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}A_{\alpha}
.

The Einstein vacuum equations for
(I\times\mathbb{R}^{3},^{(4)}g)
are thus equivalent to the following system of equations :

(2.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Box_{g}\varphi=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\omega\\
\Box_{g}\omega=4\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\varphi\\
R_{\mu\nu}(g)=2\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{\mu}\omega\partial_{\nu}\omega\end{array}.\right.

Solving the system (
2.2
) is the goal of this article. Note that the last equation of (
2.2
) is actually the Einstein equation
G_{\mu\nu}(g)=T_{\mu\nu}
with the following stress-energy-momentum tensor :

(2.3)

T_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\left(2\partial_{\mu}\omega\partial_{\nu}\omega-g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega\right).

2.3.
The elliptic gauge

In this section, we present the elliptic gauge. We first write the
(2+1)
-dimensional metric
g
on
\mathcal{M}\vcentcolon=I\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the usual form :

(2.4)

g=-N^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}+\bar{g}_{ij}\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}+\beta^{i}\mathrm{d}t\right)\left(\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\beta^{j}\mathrm{d}t\right).

Let
\Sigma_{t}\vcentcolon=\left\{(s,x)\in\mathcal{M}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ s=t\right\}
and
e_{0}\vcentcolon=\partial_{t}-\beta^{i}\partial_{i}
, which is a future directed normal to
\Sigma_{t}
. The function
N
is called the lapse and the vector field
\beta
is the shift. We introduce
\mathbf{T}\vcentcolon=\frac{e_{0}}{N}
, the unit future directed normal to
\Sigma_{t}
.
We introduce the second fundamental form of the embedding
\Sigma_{t}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{M}

(2.5)

K_{ij}\vcentcolon=-\frac{1}{2N}\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}\bar{g}_{ij}.

We decompose
K
into its trace and traceless part :

(2.6)

K_{ij}=H_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{g}_{ij}\tau,

where
\tau=\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}K
. We introduce the following gauge conditions, which define the elliptic gauge :

•

\bar{g}
is conformally flat, i.e there exists a function
\gamma
such that

(2.7)

\bar{g}_{ij}=e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}.

•

the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
are maximal, which means that
K
is traceless, i.e

(2.8)

\tau=0.

Thus, the metric takes the following form :

(2.9)

g=-N^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}+e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}+\beta^{i}\mathrm{d}t\right)\left(\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\beta^{j}\mathrm{d}t\right).

The main computations in the elliptic gauge are performed in Appendix
A
. They show that (
2.2
) is schematically of the form

(2.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\Box_{g}U=(\partial U)^{2}\\
\Delta g=(\partial U)^{2}+(\partial g)^{2}\end{array}\right.

where
U
denotes either
\varphi
or
\omega
and in the second equation
g
denotes any metric coefficient.

3.
Main results

3.1.
Initial data

We now describe our choice of initial data for the system (
2.2
). We distinguish the
admissible
initial data, and the
admissible free
initial data.

For the rest of this paper, we choose a fixed smooth cutoff function
\chi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\chi_{|[-1,1]}=0
and
\chi_{|[-2,2]}=0
. The notation
\chi\ln
stands for the function
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\longmapsto\chi(|x|)\ln(|x|)
.

Definition 3.1

(Admissible initial data)
.

For
-1<\delta<0
and
R>0
, an admissible initial data set with respect to the elliptic gauge for (
2.2
) consists of

(1)

A conformally flat intrinsic metric
(e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij})_{|\Sigma_{0}}
which admits a decomposition

\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma},

where
\alpha\geq 0
is a constant and
\tilde{\gamma}\in H^{4}_{\delta}
.

(2)

A second fundamental form
(H_{ij})_{|\Sigma_{0}}\in H^{3}_{\delta+1}
which is traceless.

(3)

\left(\mathbf{T}\varphi,\nabla\varphi\right)_{|\Sigma_{0}}\in H^{2}
, compactly supported in
B_{R}
.

(4)

\left(\mathbf{T}\omega,\nabla\omega\right)_{|\Sigma_{0}}\in H^{2}
, compactly supported in
B_{R}
.

(5)

\gamma
and
H
are required to satisfy the following constraint equations :

\displaystyle\partial^{i}H_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi+2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\omega\partial_{j}\omega,

\displaystyle\Delta\gamma

\displaystyle=-\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{2}|H|^{2}-e^{2\gamma}\left(\mathbf{T}\varphi\right)^{2}-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}-\frac{e^{-4\varphi}}{4}\left(e^{-2\gamma}\left(\mathbf{T}\omega\right)^{2}+|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right).

We recall that the constraint equations for the Einstein Vacuum Equations are
G_{00}=T_{00}
and
G_{0i}=T_{0i}
, where
G_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}
is the Einstein tensor, and
T_{\mu\nu}
is the stress-energy-momentum tensor associated to the matter fields
\varphi
and
\omega
, according to the RHS of system (
2.2
). The fact that these equations reduces in the elliptic gauge to the previous equations on
H
and
\gamma
can be proved using the computations done in Appendix
A
.

We define the notion of admissible free initial data as follows :

Definition 3.2

(Admissible free initial data)
.

We set
\mathring{\varphi}=e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\varphi
and
\mathring{\omega}=e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\omega
, where
\gamma
is as in (
2.9
). For
-1<\delta<0
and
R>0
, an admissible free initial data set with respect to the elliptic gauge for (
2.2
) is given by
(\mathring{\varphi},\nabla\varphi)_{|\Sigma_{0}}\in H^{2}
and
(\mathring{\omega},\nabla\omega)_{|\Sigma_{0}}\in H^{2}
, all compactly supported in
B_{R}
, satisfying

(3.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(2\mathring{\varphi}\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}\partial_{j}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x=0,\quad j=1,2.

The interest of the admissible free initial data is that we can construct from them a set of admissible initial data, which in particular satisfies the constraint equations. Note that instead of prescribing
\mathbf{T}\varphi
and
\mathbf{T}\omega
, we prescribe a suitable rescaled version of them, which allows the decoupling of the two constraint equations : we will first solve for
H
and then for
\gamma
.

3.2.
Statement of the theorems

The following is our main result on local well-posedness for (
2.2
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
-1<\delta<0
and
R>0
. Given an admissible free initial data set such that

\left\|\mathring{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}\leq\varepsilon,

and

C_{high}\vcentcolon=\left\|\mathring{\varphi}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{H^{2}}<\infty,

for any
C_{high}
, there exists a constant
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\delta,R)>0
independent of
C_{high}
and a time
T=T(C_{high},\delta,R)>0
such that, if
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists a unique solution to (
2.2
) in elliptic gauge on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Moreover, defining
\delta^{\prime}=\delta-\varepsilon
, there exists a constant
C_{h}=C_{h}(C_{high},\delta,R)>0
such that

•

The fields
\varphi
and
\omega
satisfy for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\varphi\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\omega\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\omega\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

and their supports are both included in
J^{+}\left(\Sigma_{0}\cap B_{R}\right)
, where
J^{+}
denotes the causal future.

•

The metric components
\gamma
and
N
can be decomposed as

\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma},\quad N=1+N_{a}\chi\ln+\tilde{N},

with
\alpha\geq 0
and
N_{a}(t)\geq 0
a function of
t
alone.

•

\gamma
,
N
and
\beta
satisfy the following estimates for all
t\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle|\alpha|+\left\|\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

\displaystyle\left|N_{a}\right|+\left|\partial_{t}N_{a}\right|+\left|\partial_{t}^{2}N_{a}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

\displaystyle\left\|\beta\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\beta\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\beta\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h}.

•

The following conservation laws hold :

(3.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(4e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+e^{-4\varphi+2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\omega\partial_{j}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=0,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(2e^{-2\gamma}|H|^{2}+4e^{2\gamma}(\mathbf{T}\varphi)^{2}+e^{-4\varphi+2\gamma}(\mathbf{T}\omega)^{2}+4|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+e^{-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=4\alpha.

This theorem has the following corollary, which basically states that if we want to have
T=1
, it suffices to take
C_{high}
small enough. We will omit the details of its proof because it is actually simplier than the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Corollary 3.1
.

Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
hold. There exists
\varepsilon_{small}=\varepsilon_{small}(\delta,R)>0
such that if
C_{high}
and
\varepsilon
in Theorem
3.1
satisfy

C_{high},\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{small},

then the unique solution exists in
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Moreover, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta,R)
such that all the estimates in Theorem
3.1
hold with
C_{h}
replaced by
C_{0}\varepsilon
.

We also prove the following theorem, which is a blow-up criterium (See the introduction of Section
7
for a discussion of this theorem) :

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
T>0
be the maximal time of existence of the solution of (
2.2
) obtained in Theorem
3.1
. If
T<+\infty
and
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough (still independent of
C_{high}
) then one of the following holds :

i)

\sup_{[0,T)}\left(\left\|\partial\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial\omega\right\|_{H^{1}}\right)=+\infty
,

ii)

\sup_{[0,T)}\left(\left\|\partial\varphi\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\partial\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}\right)>\varepsilon_{0}
,

As said in the introduction, one major feature of these two theorems is that the smallness constant
\varepsilon_{0}
does not depend on
C_{high}
. Note in contrast that the time of existence
T
does depend on
C_{high}
.

3.3.
The reduced system

In order to solve the Einstein Equations, we will first solve the following system, which we call the reduced system. It is identical to the one introduced in
[
8
]
.

(3.4)

\displaystyle\Delta N

\displaystyle=e^{-2\gamma}N|H|^{2}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}N+\frac{2e^{2\gamma}}{N}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}+\frac{e^{2\gamma-4\varphi}}{2N}(e_{0}\omega)^{2},

(3.5)

\displaystyle L\beta

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\gamma}NH,

(3.6)

\displaystyle N\tau

\displaystyle=-2e_{0}\gamma+\mathrm{div}\beta,

\displaystyle e_{0}H_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2e^{-2\gamma}NH_{i}^{\;\,\ell}H_{j\ell}+\partial_{(j}\beta^{k}H_{i)k}-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{i}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j})N+(\delta_{i}^{k}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\gamma)\partial_{k}N

(3.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-(\partial_{i}\varphi\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\varphi)N-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}(\partial_{i}\omega\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\omega)N,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{2}\gamma-e^{-2\gamma}\Delta\gamma

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{T}\left(\frac{\mathrm{div}(\beta)}{N}\right)+e^{-2\gamma}\left(\frac{\Delta N}{2N}+|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\right),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{2}\varphi-e^{-2\gamma}\Delta\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla N+\tau\mathbf{T}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\left((e_{0}\omega)^{2}+|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{T}^{2}\omega-e^{-2\gamma}\Delta\omega

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla N+\tau\mathbf{T}\omega-4e_{0}\omega e_{0}\varphi-4\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi,

where we use the notation
u_{i}\bar{\otimes}v_{j}=u_{i}v_{j}+u_{j}v_{i}-\delta_{ij}u^{k}v_{k}
.

Let us explain where equations (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) come from :

•

Considering (
A.11
) and (
A.22
), the equation
R_{00}=T_{00}-g_{00}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T
without the term in
e_{0}\tau
gives (
3.4
).

•

For
\beta
and
\tau
, the equations (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) simply come from (
A.7
) and (
A.8
).

•

To obtain the equation for
H
, we basically take the traceless part of
R_{ij}
. More precisely, using (
A.9
), (
A.12
), (
A.22
) and (
A.24
) the equation

R_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\delta^{k\ell}R_{k\ell}=T_{ij}-g_{ij}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\left(T_{k\ell}-g_{k\ell}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T\right)

gives (
3.7
).

•

Considering (
A.12
) and (
A.23
), the equation
\delta^{ij}R_{ij}=\delta^{ij}(T_{ij}-g_{ij}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T)
reads :

\Delta\gamma=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\mathbf{T}\tau-\frac{\Delta N}{2N}-\left|\nabla\varphi\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}.

Using (
A.8
), we can compute
\mathbf{T}\tau
and inject it in the previous equation to obtain (
3.8
).

•

For the equation on the matter fields
\varphi
and on
\omega
, we simply use Proposition
A.1
to rewrites
\Box_{g}\varphi
and
\Box_{g}\omega
.

After obtaining a solution to the reduced system, our next task will be to prove that this solution is in fact a solution of (
2.2
).

Note that
H
and
\gamma
no longer satisfy elliptic equations, whereas in the ”full” Einstein equations in the elliptic gauge they do. We follow this strategy to avoid to propagate the two conservation laws (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), which would have been essential for solving elliptic equations and obtain a suitable behavior at spacelike infinity for
H
and
\gamma
. Since we assume these conservation laws to hold initially, we do obtain this behavior while solving the constraints equations.

Therefore, only
N
and
\beta
satisfy elliptic equations, and the reduced system is a coupled hyperbolic-elliptic-transport system.

3.4.
Outline of the proof

We briefly discuss the structure of this article, which aims at proving Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
.

First of all, in Section
4
, we solve the constraints equations. More precisely we prove that an admissible free initial data set gives rise to an actual admissible initial data set, thus satisfying the constraints equations. Then, we split the proof of Theorem
3.1
into two parts :

•

in Section
5
, we solve the reduced system (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) using an iteration scheme, with initial data given by Section
4
. During this iteration scheme, we first prove that our sequence of approximate solution is uniformaly bounded (see Section
5.2
) and then that it is a Cauchy sequence (see Section
5.3
).

•

in Section
6
, we prove that the solution to the reduced system is indeed a solution to
(
2.2
)
and that it satisfies all the estimates stated in Theorem
3.1
.

We prove Theorem
3.2
in Section
7
, using a continuity argument based on a special energy estimate which suits the wave map structure of the coupled system satisfied by
\varphi
and
\omega
.

Finally, this article contains two appendices :

•

Appendix
A
presents the computations of the connection coefficients and the Ricci tensor in the elliptic gauge, as well as some formulae related to the stress-energy-momentum tensor.

•

Appendix
B
presents the main tools regarding the spaces
W^{m,p}_{\delta}
: embeddings results, product laws, and a theorem due to McOwen which allows us to solve elliptic equations on thoses spaces. It ends with some standard inequalities used in the proof.

4.
Initial data and the constraints equations

In this section, we follow
[
6
]
and discuss the initial data for the reduced system, and in particular we solve the constraints equations. More precisely, we will show that an admissible free initial data set gives rise to a unique admissible initial data set satisfying the constraint equations.

We will then derive the initial data for
N
and
\beta
and prove their regularity properties. Note that, since
\mathring{\varphi}
and
\mathring{\omega}
are prescribed, once we have the initial data for
N
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, we obtain the initial data for
\partial_{t}\varphi
and
\partial_{t}\omega
.

We will only care about highlighting the dependence on
\varepsilon
and
C_{high}
in the following estimates and will use the notation
\lesssim
where the implicit constant only depends on
\delta
,
R
or on any constants coming from embeddings results.

Before we go into solving the constraint equations, let us prove a simple lemma which will allows us to deal with the
e^{-4\varphi}
and
e^{\pm 2\gamma}
factors, which will occur many times in the equations.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma}
be a function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
0\leq\alpha\leq 1
,
\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq 1
and
\varphi\in H^{3}
a compactly supported function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\left\|\varphi\right\|_{W^{1,4}}\leq\varepsilon
. Then, for all functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, the following estimates holds for
k=0,1,2
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-2\gamma}f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\nu+2\alpha}},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{2\gamma}f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{k}_{\nu}},

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-4\varphi}f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k}_{\nu}}+k\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{H^{2}}\left\|f\right\|_{H^{k^{\prime}-1}}+k(k-1)\left\|\nabla^{2}\varphi f\right\|_{L^{2}}.

Moreover, if in addition
\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}<\infty
, the following estimate holds :

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-2\gamma}f\right\|_{H^{3}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{3}_{\nu+2\alpha}}+\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\|f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha}},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\left\|e^{2\gamma}f\right\|_{H^{3}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{3}_{\nu}}+\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\|f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu}}.

Proof.

We recall the embedding
H^{2}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
, which implies that
\left|e^{-2\tilde{\gamma}}\right|\lesssim 1
, which allows us to forget about these factors in the following computations. Similarly, we have
\left|e^{2\alpha\chi\ln}\right|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{2\alpha}
, which will be responsible for the change of decrease order (this remark also implies that proving (
4.1
) and (
4.4
) is enough to get (
4.2
) and (
4.5
)).

Moreover, we only prove (
4.4
), since it will be clear that its proof will include the proof of (
4.1
). With these remarks in mind, we compute directly :

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-2\gamma}f\right\|_{H^{3}_{\nu}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{H^{3}_{\nu+2\alpha}}+\left\|\nabla\gamma f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}}+\left\|\nabla^{2}\gamma f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\left(\nabla\gamma\right)^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}+\left\|\nabla\gamma\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}+\left\|\nabla^{2}\gamma\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\left(\nabla\gamma\right)^{2}\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}+\left\|\nabla\gamma\nabla^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\nabla^{3}\gamma f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}+\left\|\nabla\gamma\nabla^{2}\gamma f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}+\left\|\left(\nabla\gamma\right)^{3}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}

Because of
\left|\nabla^{a}(\chi\ln)\right|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-|a|}
(which is valid for every multi-index
a\neq 0
), we can forget about the
\chi\ln
part in
\gamma
and pretend that
\gamma
is replaced by
\tilde{\gamma}
. Using the product estimate (see Proposition
B.2
), we can deal with all these terms :

\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\|f\|_{H^{1}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}},\quad

\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\|f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha}}
,

\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right)^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}^{2}\|f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha}},\quad

\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\|\nabla f\|_{H^{1}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}}
,

\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\|\nabla f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}},\quad

\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right)^{2}\nabla f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}^{2}\|\nabla f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+1}},

\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\nabla^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\left\|\nabla^{2}f\right\|_{H^{1}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}},\quad

\left\|\nabla^{3}\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{3}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}\|f\|_{H^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha}},

\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}},\quad

\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right)^{3}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}\lesssim\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right)^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}.

Note that the last two estimates involve
\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+2}}
and
\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right)^{2}f\right\|_{L^{2}_{\nu+2\alpha+3}}
, which have already been estimated. Looking at these estimates, we see that the only ones which uses
\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}
are the three last ones. Those terms don’t appear if we only differentiate twice or less, it is therefore clear why (
4.1
) is also proved. The proof of (
4.3
) is identical, using the embeddings
W^{1,4}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
and
H^{2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
.
∎

4.1.
The constraints equations

We are now ready to solve the constraints equations, which we rewrite in terms of
\mathring{\varphi}
and
\mathring{\omega}
:

(4.6)

\displaystyle\partial^{i}H_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2\mathring{\varphi}\partial_{j}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}\partial_{j}\omega,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Delta\gamma

\displaystyle=-e^{-2\gamma}\left(\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|H|^{2}\right)-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}.

Lemma 4.2
.

The equation (
4.6
) admits a unique solution
H\in H^{3}_{\delta+1}
, a symmetric traceless covariant 2-tensor with
\|H\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

We look for a solution under the form
H=LY
where
Y
is a 1-form. We have
\partial^{i}H_{ij}=\Delta Y_{j}
and
Y
solves

\Delta Y_{j}=-2\mathring{\varphi}\partial_{j}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}\partial_{j}\omega.

Using the definition of
L
, it’s easy to check that
LY
is a traceless symmetric 2-tensor. We use the Theorem
B.1
in the case
p=2
and
m=0
, the range of the Laplacian is then the functions
f\in H^{0}_{\delta+2}
such that
\int f=0
. By assumption,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(-2\mathring{\varphi}\partial_{j}\varphi-\frac{e^{-4\varphi}}{2}\mathring{\omega}\partial_{j}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x=0
and thanks to the support property, the Hölder inequality and (
4.3
) we have :

\left\|\Delta Y_{j}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\|\mathring{\varphi}\partial_{j}\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}\partial_{j}\omega\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\mathring{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

Thus, there exists a unique solution
Y_{j}\in H^{2}_{\delta}
. Moreover we have
\|Y_{j}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}
, which implies
\|H\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}
.

We can improve the regularity of
H
, by noting that

\|H\|_{H^{3}_{\delta+1}}\leq\|Y\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}\lesssim\|\mathring{\varphi}\nabla\varphi\|_{H^{2}}+\|\mathring{\omega}\nabla\omega\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim C_{high}^{2}.

In the last inequality we use the fact that in dimension 2,
H^{2}
is an algebra.

Our solution
H\in H^{3}_{\delta+1}
is unique, because of the following fact : if
H\in H^{3}_{\delta+1}
is a traceless symmetric divergence free 2-tensor, we have componentwise
\Delta H_{ij}=0
, which implies
H=0
, again thanks to Theorem
B.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

For
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the equation (
4.7
) admits a unique solution
\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma}
with
\tilde{\gamma}\in H^{4}_{\delta}
,
\|\tilde{\gamma}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
and
0\leq\alpha\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

We are going to use a fixed point argument in
[0,\varepsilon]\times B_{H^{2}_{\delta}}(0,\varepsilon)
. We define on this space the application
\phi:(\alpha^{(1)},\tilde{\gamma}^{(1)})\longmapsto(\alpha^{(2)},\tilde{\gamma}^{(2)})
, where
\gamma^{(2)}
is the unique solution of

(4.8)

\Delta\gamma^{(2)}=-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}-e^{-2\gamma^{(1)}}\left(\frac{1}{2}|H|^{2}+\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right),

with the notation
\gamma^{(i)}=-\alpha^{(i)}\chi(r)\ln(r)+\tilde{\gamma}^{(i)}
. We want to prove that if
\varepsilon
is small enough,
\phi
is indeed a contraction.
Let us show that the RHS of (
4.8
) is in
H^{0}_{\delta+2}
. By assumption on
\varphi
and
\omega
we can write, using Hölder’s inequality and (
4.3
) :

\left\||\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\||\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\||\nabla\omega|^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

For the term
e^{-2\gamma^{(1)}}|H|^{2}
, we use (
4.1
), the product estimate and choose
\varepsilon
small enough :

\left\|e^{-2\gamma^{(1)}}|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\||H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2(1+\varepsilon)}}\lesssim\|H\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}.

The last terms is handled with the same arguments :

\left\|e^{-2\gamma^{(1)}}\left(\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\|\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

We next prove the bound on
\alpha^{(2)}=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\text{RHS of \eqref{contraction 1}})
, its positivity being clear. We have

\displaystyle\left|\alpha^{(2)}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|e^{-\gamma^{(1)}}H\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|e^{-\gamma^{(1)}}\mathring{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|e^{-\gamma^{(1)}-2\varphi}\omega\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla\varphi\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\left\|\nabla\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\left\|H\right\|_{H^{0}_{\varepsilon}}^{2}+\left\|\mathring{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},

where we used Hölder’s inequality, (
4.1
) (for the three last terms) and the support property of
\varphi
,
\mathring{\varphi}
,
\omega
and
\mathring{\omega}
. In conclusion, thanks to Corollary
B.1
, if
\varepsilon
is small enough,
\phi
is indeed an application from
[0,\varepsilon]\times B_{H^{2}_{\delta}}(0,\varepsilon)
to itself and we can prove in the same way that this is a contraction.

We can improve the regularity of
\tilde{\gamma}
, using (
4.1
) and (
4.3
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|e^{-2\gamma}H^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2}}+\left\|e^{-2\gamma}\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}++\left\||\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|H^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2\varepsilon+2}}+\left\|\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\||\nabla\varphi|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|H\|_{H^{3}_{\delta+1}}^{2}+C_{high}^{2},

where in the last inequality, we used the product estimate (with
\varepsilon
small enough) for the first term and the algebraic structure of
H^{2}
for the remaining terms. Thanks to Lemma
4.2
, the final quantity is finite, which concludes the proof.

∎

4.2.
Initial data to the reduced system

The equations satisfied by
N
and
\beta
are :

(4.9)

\displaystyle\Delta N

\displaystyle=e^{-2\gamma}N\left(|H|^{2}+\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right),

(4.10)

\displaystyle L\beta

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\gamma}NH.

The equation (
4.9
) comes from (
3.4
) in the case
\tau=0
, and the equation (
4.10
) comes from (
A.7
).

Lemma 4.4
.

For
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the equation (
4.9
) admits a unique solution
N=1+N_{a}\chi\ln+\tilde{N}
with
\tilde{N}\in H^{4}_{\delta}
,
\|\tilde{N}\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
and
0\leq N_{a}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

We look for a solution of the form
N=1+N_{a}\chi(r)\ln(r)+\tilde{N}
, with
N_{a}\geq 0
. On the space
[0,\varepsilon]\times B_{H^{2}_{\delta}}(0,\varepsilon)
, we define the application
\phi(N_{a}^{(1)},\tilde{N}^{(1)})=(N_{a}^{(2)},\tilde{N}^{(2)})
where (with the notation
N^{(i)}=1+N_{a}^{(i)}\chi(r)\ln(r)+\tilde{N}^{(i)}
),
N^{(2)}
is the solution of

(4.11)

\Delta N^{(2)}=e^{-2\gamma}N^{(1)}(|H|^{2}+\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}).

Let’s show that the RHS is in
H^{0}_{\delta+2}
.
Thanks to the support property of
\mathring{\varphi}
and
\mathring{\omega}
, the first term is handled quite easily using (
4.1
), (
4.3
) and the fact that
\left\|N^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R})}\lesssim 1
(note the embedding
H^{2}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
) :

\left\|e^{-2\gamma}N^{(1)}\left(\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\|N^{(1)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R})}\left(\left\|\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}.

Using again (
4.1
), the fact that
|\chi\ln|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{\frac{\delta+1}{2}}
, the embedding
H^{2}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
(used for
\tilde{N}^{(1)}
) and the product estimate, we handle the second term :

\displaystyle\left\|e^{-2\gamma}N^{(1)}|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|N^{(1)}|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\||H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon}}\left(1+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\right)+\varepsilon\left\||H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon+\frac{\delta+1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\varepsilon)\left\|H\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}.

We showed that, for
\varepsilon
small enough, we have
\|\Delta N^{(2)}\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

We have :

(4.12)

2\pi N_{a}^{(2)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\gamma}N^{(1)}|H|^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\gamma}N^{(1)}\mathring{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\gamma-4\varphi}N^{(1)}\mathring{\omega}^{2}.

If
\varepsilon
is small enough, we have
N^{(1)}\geq 0
(using the embedding
H^{2}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
) so that
N_{a}^{(2)}\geq 0
. With the same kind of arguments than previously, we can easily show that
N_{a}^{(2)}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

This concludes the fact that
\phi
is well defined (providing
\varepsilon
is small and thanks to Corollary
B.1
), and that this is a contraction (the calculations are likewise, since the equation is linear).

We can improve the regularity of
\tilde{N}
, using (
4.1
) and (
4.3
) :

(4.13)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|e^{-2\gamma}N|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2}}+\left\|e^{-2\gamma}N\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|e^{-2\gamma-4\varphi}N\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\||H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon}}\left(1+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(1)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\right)+\varepsilon\left\|\chi\ln|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon}}+\left\|N\right\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R})}\left(\left\|\mathring{\varphi}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\mathring{\omega}^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}}\right).

Using
|\chi\ln|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{\frac{\delta+1}{2}}
and
\left|\nabla^{a}(\chi\ln)\right|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-|a|}
(for
a\neq 0
), we easily show that
\left\|\chi\ln|H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon}}\lesssim\left\||H|^{2}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon+\frac{\delta+1}{2}}}
to obtain :

\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}\lesssim(1+\varepsilon)\left\|H\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta+1}}^{2}+\|\mathring{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}}^{2}+\|\mathring{\omega}\|_{H^{2}}^{2},

which is finite, thanks to Lemma
4.2
.
∎

The following simple lemma will be useful in order to use Theorem
B.1
for
\beta
:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
and
u=(u_{1},u_{2})
be a fonction from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
u_{i}\in H^{m}_{\nu}
. If
m\geq 2
and
\nu>0
, then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{div}(u)=0

Proof.

We fix
R>0
and use the Stokes formula :

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{B_{R}}\mathrm{div}(u)\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{\partial B_{R}}u.n\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\bigg|\leq\int_{\partial B_{R}}\langle x\rangle^{-\nu-1}\langle x\rangle^{\nu+1}|u.n|\,\mathrm{d}\sigma\lesssim\|u\|_{C^{0}_{\nu+1}}R^{-\nu}

If
m\geq 2
and
\nu>0
we have the Sobolev embeddings
H^{m}_{\nu}\subset C^{0}_{\nu+1}
, which concludes the proof since the last inequality implies

\lim_{R\to+\infty}\int_{B_{R}}\mathrm{div}(u)=0.

∎

Lemma 4.6
.

For
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, the equation (
4.10
) admits a unique solution
\beta\in H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}
with
\|\beta\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

We take the divergence of (
4.10
) to obtain the following elliptic equation :

(4.14)

\Delta\beta_{j}=\partial^{i}(2Ne^{-2\gamma}H_{ij})

Thanks to Lemma
4.5
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial^{i}(2Ne^{-2\gamma}H_{ij})=0
(the fact that
e^{-2\gamma}NH\in H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}
will be proved in the sequel of this proof). Thus, in order to apply Theorem
B.1
, it remains to show that
\|\partial^{i}(2Ne^{-2\gamma}H_{ij})\|_{H^{0}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
. For that, we use (
4.1
),
\varepsilon|\chi\ln|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}
, Lemmas
4.2
and
4.4
:

\displaystyle\left\|\partial^{i}(2Ne^{-2\gamma}H_{ij})\right\|_{H^{0}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|e^{-2\gamma}NH\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|H\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\left(1+\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\right)+\left\|H\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+C\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},

where in the last inequality, we take
\varepsilon
such that
C\varepsilon^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Thus, we can apply Theorem
B.1
to obtain the existence of a solution to (
4.14
). We can improve the regularity of this solution using (
4.4
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\beta\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|e^{-2\gamma}NH\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|NH\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}+C\varepsilon^{2}+1}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left\|NH\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+C\varepsilon^{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\right)\left(\left\|H\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta+1}}\left(1+\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}\right)+\left\|H\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+C\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}}\right).

Taking
\varepsilon
such that
C\varepsilon^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
, we conclude using Lemmas
4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
that
\left\|\beta\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}}<\infty
.

It remains to show that our solution
\beta
satisfies
L\beta=2Ne^{-2\gamma}H
. We have shown that
L\beta-2Ne^{-2\gamma}H
is a covariant symmetric traceless divergence free 2-tensor, it implies that its components are harmonic, and thus vanishes (because they belong to
H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}
). We use the same argument to show that the solution is unique.
∎

In order to have
\tau_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
, we must have the following :

Lemma 4.7
.

We set
e_{0}\gamma=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{div}(\beta)
. Then, we have
e_{0}\gamma\in H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}
and
\|e_{0}\gamma\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}
.

Proof.

It follows directly from the estimates on
\beta
proved in Lemma
4.6
and from Lemma
B.1
.
∎

We summarise in the next corollary our results about the constraints equations and the initial data :

Corollary 4.1
.

For
\varepsilon
sufficiently small depending only on
\delta
, given a free initial data set, there exists an initial data set to the reduced system such that the constraints equations are satisfied and
\tau_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
. Moreover, we have the following estimates :

•

there exists
C>0
depending only on
\delta
and
R
such that :

(4.15)

\|H\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}+|\alpha|+\left\|\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}+\|e_{0}\gamma\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+|N_{a}|+\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}+\|\beta\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

•

there exists
C_{i}>0
depending on
\delta
,
R
and
C_{high}
such that :

(4.16)

\|H\|_{H^{3}_{\delta+1}}+\left\|\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}+\|e_{0}\gamma\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left\|\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}+\|\beta\|_{H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}}\leq C_{i}

5.
Solving the reduced system

In this section, we solve the reduced system of equations introduced in Section
3.3
by an iteration methode. We first prove that we can construct a sequence, defined in Section
5.1
and bounded in a small space (this is done in Section
5.2
). Then we prove in Section
5.3
that the sequence is Cauchy in a larger space, which will imply the existence and uniqueness of solutions to the reduced system of equations.

5.1.
Iteration scheme

In order to solve the reduced system (
3.4
)-(
3.9
), we construct the sequence

(N^{(n)}=1+N_{a}^{(n)}\chi\ln+\tilde{N}^{(n)},\tau^{(n)},H^{(n)},\beta^{(n)},\gamma^{(n)}=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)},\varphi^{(n)},\omega^{(n)})

iteratively as follows : for
n=1,2
, let
N^{(n)},\tau^{(n)},H^{(n)},\beta^{(n)},\gamma^{(n)},\varphi^{(n)}
be time-independent, with initial data as in Section
4
. For
n\geq 2
, given the
n
-th iterate, the
(n+1)
-st iterate is then defined by solving the following system with initial data as in Section
4
:

\displaystyle\Delta N^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left|H^{(n)}\right|^{2}+\frac{\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}

(5.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad+\frac{2e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)^{2}+\frac{e^{2\gamma^{(n)}-4\varphi^{(n)}}}{2N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right)^{2}

(5.2)

\displaystyle L\beta^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}H^{(n)}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\tau^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\gamma^{(n)}+\frac{\mathrm{div}\left(\beta^{(n)}\right)}{N^{(n-1)}}

\displaystyle e_{0}^{(n+1)}\left(H^{(n+1)}\right)_{ij}

\displaystyle=-2e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left(H^{(n)}\right)_{i}^{\;\,\ell}\left(H^{(n)}\right)_{j\ell}+\partial_{(j}\left(\beta^{(n)}\right)^{k}\left(H^{(n)}\right)_{i)k}

(5.4)

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{i}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\right)N^{(n)}+\left(\delta_{i}^{k}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\gamma^{(n)}\right)\partial_{k}N^{(n)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\partial_{i}\varphi^{(n)}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\varphi^{(n)}\right)N^{(n)}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi^{(n)}}\left(\partial_{i}\omega^{(n)}\bar{\otimes}\partial_{j}\omega^{(n)}\right)N^{(n)}

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\gamma^{(n+1)}-e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}\Delta\gamma^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{2N^{(n)}}e_{0}^{(n-1)}\left(\frac{\mathrm{div}\left(\beta^{(n)}\right)}{N^{(n-1)}}\right)

(5.5)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad+e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}\left(\frac{\Delta N^{(n)}}{2N^{(n)}}+\left|\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi^{(n)}}\left|\nabla\omega^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\varphi^{(n+1)}-e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}\Delta\varphi^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\cdot\nabla N^{(n)}+\frac{\tau^{(n)}e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}}{N^{(n)}}

(5.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi^{(n)}}\left(\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right)^{2}+\left|\nabla\omega^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\right)^{2}\omega^{(n+1)}-e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}\Delta\omega^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\nabla\omega^{(n)}\cdot\nabla N^{(n)}+\frac{\tau^{(n)}e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}}{N^{(n)}}

(5.7)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-4e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}-4\nabla\omega^{(n)}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{(n)},

This system is not a linear system in the
(n+1)
-th iterate, because of the term
e_{0}^{(n+1)}H^{(n+1)}
in (
5.4
) (which contains
\beta^{(n+1)}\cdot\nabla H^{(n+1)}
). The local well-posedness of this system follows from the estimates we are about to prove. Note that we use the following notation :

e_{0}^{(k)}=\partial_{t}-\nabla\beta^{(k)}\cdot\nabla\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{T}^{(k)}=\frac{e_{0}^{(k)}}{N^{(k)}}.

5.2.
Boundedness of the sequence

The first step is to show that the sequence is uniformly bounded in appropriate function spaces. We proceed by strong induction and suppose that the following estimates hold for all
k
up to some
n\geq 2
and for all
t\in[0,T]
. Here,
A_{0}\ll A_{1}\ll A_{2}\ll A_{3}\ll A_{4}
are all sufficiently large constants independent of
\varepsilon
or
n
to be choosen later. We also set
\delta^{\prime}=\delta-\varepsilon
and take
\varepsilon
small enough so that
-1<\delta^{\prime}
. We also choose
\lambda>0
a small constant such that
\lambda<\delta+1
.

•

N^{(k)}
is of the form
N^{(k)}=1+N_{a}^{(k)}\chi\ln+\tilde{N}^{(k)}
with
N_{a}^{(k)}\geq 0
and

(5.8)

\displaystyle\left|N_{a}^{(k)}\right|+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}N_{a}^{(k)}\right|+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{N}^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{i},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{N}^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}C_{i}^{2}.

•

\beta^{(k)}
satisfies

(5.11)

\displaystyle\left\|\beta^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\mathbf{\delta^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left\|\beta^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\mathbf{\delta^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}C_{i},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla e_{0}^{(k-1)}\beta^{(k)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{i},

(5.14)

\displaystyle\left\|e_{0}^{(k-1)}\beta^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{1}C_{i},

(5.15)

\displaystyle\left\|e_{0}^{(k-1)}\beta^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{4}C_{i}^{2}.

•

H^{(k)}
satisfies

(5.16)

\displaystyle\left\|H^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+1}}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{i},

(5.17)

\displaystyle\left\|e_{0}^{(k)}H^{(k)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{1+\lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left\|e_{0}^{(k)}H^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}C_{i},

(5.19)

\displaystyle\left\|e_{0}^{(k)}H^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{3}C_{i}^{2}.

•

\tau^{(k)}
satisfies

(5.20)

\displaystyle\left\|\tau^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{1}C_{i},

(5.21)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\tau^{(k)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}C_{i},

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\tau^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{3}C_{i}.

•

\gamma^{(k)}
is of the form
\gamma^{(k)}=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}
with
\alpha
as previously and
\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}
satisfies

(5.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(k-1)}\nabla^{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+|\alpha|}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq 8C_{i},

(5.24)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(k-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}C_{i},

(5.25)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(k-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(k)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{2}C_{i}.

•

\varphi^{(k)}
and
\omega^{(k)}
are compactly supported in

\left\{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ |x|\leq R+C_{s}(1+R^{\varepsilon})t\right\},

where
C_{s}>0
is to be choosen in Lemma
5.6
. Choosing
T
smaller if necessary, we assume that the above set is a subset of
[0,T]\times B_{2R}
. Moreover, the following estimates hold :

(5.26)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\varphi^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\varphi^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(k-1)}\varphi^{(k)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}C_{i},

(5.27)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\omega^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\omega^{(k)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(k-1)}\omega^{(k)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq A_{0}C_{i},

Recalling the statement of Theorem
3.1
,
C_{high}
is a potentially large constant on which
T
can depend, but
\varepsilon_{0}
has to be independent of
C_{high}
and
C_{i}
(which, as explained in Corollary
4.1
, depends on
C_{high}
). Therefore, in the following estimates, we will keep trace of
C_{i}
, and
\varepsilon C_{i}
is not a small constant.

We will use the symbol
\lesssim
where the implicit constants are independent of
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
,
A_{4}
and
C_{i}
and use
C
as the notation for such a constant. Moreover,
C(A_{i})
will denote a constant depending on
A_{i}
, but not on
C_{i}
. At the end of the proof, we will choose
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
and
A_{4}
such that
C(A_{i})\ll A_{i+1}
for all
i=0,\dots,3
.

Our goal now is to prove that all this estimates are still true for the next iterate. For most of these, we will in fact show that they hold with better constants on the RHS.

5.2.1.
Preliminary estimates

The next result will be very useful in the sequel :

Proposition 5.1
.

The following estimate holds :

\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\leq 9C_{i}.

Proof.

In view of (
5.23
), we have to commute
\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}
with
\nabla^{\alpha}
(for
|\alpha|\leq 2
). Indeed :

\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\leq\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\left(\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla^{(\alpha)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+|\alpha|}}+\left\|\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla^{\alpha}\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+|\alpha|}}\right)

Using the commutation formula
[e_{0}^{(n-1)},\nabla]=\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}\nabla
, we compute

\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}=\frac{\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}-\frac{\nabla N^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}

We need smallness for the metric component so we use on one hand (
5.11
) the product estimate, the fact that
|\frac{1}{N^{(n-1)}}|\lesssim 1
and on the other hand the fact that
|\nabla(\chi\ln)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-1}
and (
5.8
) to write

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left|N_{a}^{(n-1)}\right|\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n-1)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon\left(\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\right)+\varepsilon\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}.

Now we compute
\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla^{2}\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}
:

\displaystyle\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla^{2}\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}-2\frac{\nabla N^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}+\left(\frac{\nabla^{2}\beta^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}+\frac{\nabla N^{(n-1)}\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}}{\left(N^{(n-1)}\right)^{2}}\right)\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\nabla^{2}N^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}+\left(\frac{\nabla N^{(n-1)}}{N^{(n-1)}}\right)^{2}\right)\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}.

Using the product estimate and
|\frac{1}{N^{(n-1)}}|\lesssim 1
we do the following :

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla^{2}\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}\left\|\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left\|N_{a}^{(n-1)}\nabla(\chi\ln)\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n-1)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}\beta^{(n-1)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}+\left\|\nabla N^{(n-1)}\nabla\beta^{(n-1)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}N^{(n-1)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}+\left\|\left(\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n-1)}\right)^{2}+N_{a}^{(n-1)}\nabla(\chi\ln)\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n-1)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\left(N_{a}^{(n-1)}\nabla(\chi\ln)\right)^{2}\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}.

Now using (
5.8
), (
5.11
),
|\nabla(\chi\ln)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{-1}
we have :

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)},\nabla^{2}\right]\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\;\varepsilon\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla^{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+|\alpha|}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\varepsilon\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}

It remains to deal with the last term in this last inequality. Using the same type of arguments as above we can show that :

\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}\lesssim\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}+\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+3}}+\varepsilon\left\|\nabla^{2}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+2}}

Summarising, we get :

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+\varepsilon)\left(\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\nabla^{\alpha}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1+|\alpha|}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

By choosing
\varepsilon
small enough, we can absorb the last term of the RHS into the LHS and using (
5.23
) we finally prove the desired result.
∎

We continue with a propagation of smallness result.

Proposition 5.2
.

The following estimates hold for
T
sufficiently small and
C_{p}>0
a constant depending on
\delta
and
R
only :

(5.28)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\nabla\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\varepsilon,

(5.29)

\displaystyle\left\|H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\varepsilon^{2},

(5.30)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\varepsilon^{2},

(5.31)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\varepsilon^{2},

(5.32)

\displaystyle\left\|\tau^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\varepsilon^{2}.

Proof.

By Corollary
4.1
, all these quantities satisfy the desired smallness estimates at
t=0
.
The fact that these estimates are true for all
t\in[0,T]
will then follow from calculus inequalities of the type

\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\|u\|_{W^{m,p}_{\eta}}(s)\leq C^{\prime}\left(\|u\|_{W^{m,p}_{\eta}}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{t}u\|_{W^{m,p}_{\eta}}(s)\mathrm{d}s\right).

Therefore, it remains to show that the
\partial_{t}
derivatives (we recall that
\partial_{t}=e_{0}^{(n)}+\beta^{(n)}\cdot\nabla=e_{0}^{(n-1)}+\beta^{(n-1)}\cdot\nabla
) of all these terms in the relevant norms are bounded by a constant depending on
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
,
A_{4}
or
C_{i}
, and then to choose
T
small enough. We proceed as follows :

•

for
\nabla\varphi^{(n)}
and
\nabla\omega^{(n)}
, we use the embedding
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
and (
5.26
) :

(5.33)

\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla\partial_{t}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim A_{0}C_{i},

and we do the same for
\nabla\omega^{(n)}
, using (
5.27
).

•

for
\partial_{t}\varphi^{(n)}
and
\partial_{t}\omega^{(n)}
, we use the support property of
\varphi^{(n)}
, the embedding
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
, (
5.26
), (
5.11
), (
5.14
), (
5.9
) and (
5.33
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|N^{(n-1)}\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\partial_{t}N^{(n-1)}\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\beta^{(n)}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\beta^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}}\left\|\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\nabla\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim A_{0}C_{i}+\varepsilon A_{1}C_{i},

and we do the same for
\partial_{t}\omega^{(n)}
, using (
5.27
).

•

for
H^{(n)}
, we use (
5.16
), (
5.18
), (
5.11
) and the product estimate :

\left\|\partial_{t}H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\leq\left\|e_{0}^{(n)}H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}+\left\|\beta^{(n)}\nabla H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\lesssim C_{i}.

•

for
\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}
, we use (
5.1
), (
5.11
) and (
5.23
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|N^{(n-1)}\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\beta^{(n-1)}\cdot\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left\|\nabla\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}\left\|\beta^{(n-1)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{i}+A_{0}C_{i}^{2}.

•

for
\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}
and
\tau^{(n)}
, we simply use (
5.25
) and (
5.22
), which give directly the result.

∎

5.2.2.
Elliptic estimates

We begin with the two elliptic equations (the ones for
N
and
\beta
). These are the most difficult to handle, because we can’t rely on the smallness of a time parameter and therefore have to keep properly trace of the
\varepsilon
,
C_{i}
and
A_{i}
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For
n\geq 2
,
N^{(n+1)}
admits a decomposition

N^{(n+1)}=1+N_{a}^{(n+1)}\chi\ln+\tilde{N}^{(n+1)},

with
N_{a}^{(n+1)}\geq 0
and such that

(5.34)

\displaystyle\left|N_{a}^{(n+1)}\right|+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2},

(5.35)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}N_{a}^{(n+1)}\right|+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon C(A_{3})C_{i},

(5.36)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}C(A_{2})C_{i}^{2}+C(A_{0})C_{i}^{2}.

Proof.

We claim that :

\left\|\text{RHS of \eqref{reduced system N}}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

Except for the term
e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}
, all the terms in (
5.1
) can be estimated in an identical manner as in Lemma
4.4
, except that we estimate the norms using Proposition
5.2
instead of using the assumtions on the reduced data and the estimates in Lemmas
4.2
and
4.3
. It therefore remains to control
e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}
. Using (
4.2
) and (
5.8
), we see that
\left\|e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\right\|_{C^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\lesssim 1
. We finally use the product estimate and (
5.32
) to handle
\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}
:

\left\|e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left(\tau^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\|e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\right\|_{C^{0}_{\varepsilon}}\left\|\tau^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{4}.

This proves the claim. Applying Corollary
B.1
to
N^{(n+1)}-1
yields the existence of the decomposition of
N^{(n+1)}
, as well as the estimate (
5.34
).

We now turn to the proof of (
5.35
). To obtain the
H^{3}_{\delta}
bound for
\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}
, we need to control the RHS of (
5.1
) in
H^{1}_{\delta+2}
:

•

for the term
e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}|H^{(n)}|^{2}
, we do exactly the same calculations as in (
4.13
), but in
H^{1}_{\delta+2}
instead of
H^{2}_{\delta+2}
. In contrast to (
4.13
), here we have less liberty to bound the term
|H^{(n)}|^{2}
(because we need
C_{i}
and not
C_{i}^{2}
bounds), therefore we use (
5.16
) and (
5.29
) to write

\left\|e^{-2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}\left|H^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+2}}\lesssim\left\|\left|H^{(n)}\right|^{2}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+2+2\varepsilon+\frac{\delta+1}{2}}}\lesssim\left\|H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}\left\|H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta+1}}\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}C_{i}.

•

for the term
e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}(\tau^{(n)})^{2}
, we note that
\tau^{(n)}
and
H^{(n)}
satisfy the exact same estimate (according to (
5.16
), (
5.20
), (
5.29
) and (
5.32
)), except for a slight difference of weights (
\delta^{\prime}
instead of
\delta
) and constants (
A_{1}
compared to 2). Therefore we treat this term exactly as the previous one and omit the details.

•

we now discuss the term
\frac{e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)^{2}
. Since the smallness for
e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}
is at the
L^{4}
-level (thanks to (
5.28
)), any spatial derivative of
e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}
destroys the
\varepsilon
-smallness, and therefore we have to be precise. Thanks to (
4.2
), we can forget the
e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}
factor, thanks to (
5.8
) we have
\left|\frac{1}{N^{(n)}}\right|\lesssim 1
(we also forget about
\nabla(\chi\ln)
) and thanks to (
5.9
) and the embedding
H^{2}_{\delta+1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
we have
\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim C_{i}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left(1+\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)+\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\nabla\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\left(1+\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)+\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}},

where in the last inequality we used Hölder’s inequality and the Sobolev injection
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
. We now use (
5.28
) and (
5.26
) to obtain :

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}(1+C_{i})+\varepsilon A_{0}C_{i}\lesssim\varepsilon C(A_{0})C_{i}.

•

the term
\frac{e^{2\gamma^{(n)}-4\varphi^{(n)}}}{N^{(n)}}\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right)^{2}
is handled in a similar way, using first (
4.3
) to get ride of the
e^{-4\varphi^{(n)}}
factor, and then using (
5.27
) instead of (
5.26
).

This concludes the proof of the estimate
\left\|\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon C(A_{1})C_{i}
.

We now turn to the estimate for
\partial_{t}N^{(n+1)}
, including both
\partial_{t}N_{a}^{(n+1)}
and
\partial_{t}\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}
. Since the RHS of (
5.1
) is differentiable in
t
, it is easy to see that
\partial_{t}N^{(n+1)}=\partial_{t}N_{a}^{(n+1)}\chi\ln+\partial_{t}\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}
is the solution given by Corollary
B.1
to the equation

\Delta f=\partial_{t}(\text{RHS of \eqref{reduced system N}}).

Therefore, to finish the proof of (
5.35
), it suffices to bound the integral of
\partial_{t}(\text{RHS of \eqref{reduced system N}})
with respect to
\mathrm{d}x
and to bound
\partial_{t}(\text{RHS of \eqref{reduced system N}})
in
L^{2}_{\delta+2}
.

Since the estimate for
\partial_{t}\tau^{(n)}
are worse than those for
\partial_{t}H^{(n)}
, and those for
\tau^{(n)}
and
H^{(n)}
are similar, we will treat the term
\partial_{t}\left(e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}(\tau^{(n)})^{2}\right)
and leave the easier term
\partial_{t}\left(e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}|H^{(n)}|^{2}\right)
to the reader. We use (
4.2
) for the
e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}
factor and the fact that
|\chi\ln|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\left(e^{2\gamma^{(n)}}N^{(n)}(\tau^{(n)})^{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta+2}}
