\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\left\|\nabla\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}}\right)\left\|\left(e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\nabla e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{i}\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}^{2}+\left\|e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}C_{i}+\varepsilon C_{i}

•

for
VI
, we do as for
V
, since
\nabla\omega^{(n)}
and
e_{0}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}
satisfy the same estimates.

∎

Proposition 5.9
.

For
n\geq 2
, the following estimates holds :

(5.89)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\omega^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\omega^{(n+1)}\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{i},

(5.90)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\omega^{(n+1)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{i}.

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
5.9
uses the same estimates as the one of Proposition
5.8
(since
\varphi^{(n)}
and
\omega^{(n)}
satisfy the same estimates), so we omit the details.
∎

Looking at the estimates we proved for the
(n+1)
-th iterate in Propositions
5.3
,
5.4
,
5.5
,
5.6
,
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
we see that in order to recover the estimates (
5.8
)-(
5.26
), we have to choose the constants
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
and
A_{4}
such that
C(A_{i})\ll A_{i+1}
for all
i=0,\dots,3
and
\varepsilon
small, depending on the
A_{i}
constants. We make such a choice.

This concludes the proof of the fact claimed above : the sequence constructed in Section
5.1
is uniformly bounded. Moreover, the bounds (
5.8
)-(
5.27
) hold for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. and for every
t\in[0,T]
.

5.3.
Convergence of the sequence

In this section, we show that the sequence we constructed in fact converges to a limit in larger functional spaces than those used in the previous sequence, where we only proved boundedness. To this end, we will show that the sequence is a Cauchy sequence. We introduce the following distances, as in
[
8
]
:

\displaystyle d_{1}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\|\tilde{\gamma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\tilde{\gamma}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|H^{(n+1)}-H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}}

(5.91)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\tau^{(n+1)}-\tau^{(n)}\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\varphi^{(n+1)}-\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\nabla\left(\varphi^{(n+1)}-\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\omega^{(n+1)}-\omega^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\nabla\left(\omega^{(n+1)}-\omega^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}},

(5.92)

\displaystyle d_{2}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left|N_{a}^{(n+1)}-N_{a}^{(n)}\right|+\left\|\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}+\left\|\beta^{(n+1)}-\beta^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}},

(5.93)

\displaystyle d_{3}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\varphi^{(n+1)}-\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\varphi^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\omega^{(n+1)}-\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\omega^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}},

(5.94)

\displaystyle d_{4}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\|e_{0}^{(n+1)}H^{(n+1)}-e_{0}^{(n)}H^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{1}_{\delta+1}},

(5.95)

\displaystyle d_{5}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\mathbf{T}^{(n)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n+1)}-\mathbf{T}^{(n-1)}\tilde{\gamma}^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\tau^{(n+1)}-\tau^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1}},

(5.96)

\displaystyle d_{6}^{(n)}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left|\partial_{t}\left(N_{a}^{(n+1)}-N_{a}^{(n)}\right)\right|+\left\|\partial_{t}\left(\tilde{N}^{(n+1)}-\tilde{N}^{(n)}\right)\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}+\left\|e_{0}^{(n)}\beta^{(n+1)}-e_{0}^{(n-1)}\beta^{(n)}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}.

The goal is to show that each series
\sum_{n\leq 0}d_{i}^{(n)}
is converging. This is a consequence of the following Proposition. At this low-level of regularity, its proof is identical to the corresponding one done in
[
8
]
(see Proposition
8.19
and Corollary
8.20
in this article).

Proposition 5.10
.

If
T
and
\varepsilon
is small enough (where
\varepsilon
does not depend on
C_{i}
), the following bounds hold for every
n\geq 3
:

\displaystyle d_{1}^{(n)}+d_{2}^{(n)}+d_{3}^{(n)}+d_{4}^{(n)}+d_{5}^{(n)}+d_{6}^{(n)}\lesssim 2^{-n}.

It shows that in the function spaces involved in the definition of the distances
d^{(n)}_{i}
, the sequence we constructed is Cauchy and therefore convergent to some

(5.97)

(N=1+N_{a}\chi\ln+\tilde{N},\tau,H,\beta,\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma},\varphi,\omega).

Since the sequence is bounded in a smaller space, we can find a subsequence weakly converging to some limit, which has to coincide with the strong limit (
5.97
). Consequently, (
5.97
) satisfies the estimates (
5.8
)-(
5.27
), from which we can prove that it is a solution to the reduced system (
3.4
)-(
3.10
).

If there are two solutions to the reduced system, we can control their difference using the distances
d^{(n)}_{i}
and arguing as in Proposition
5.10
we show that these two solutions coincide. This proves the uniqueness of solution to the reduced system.

We summarize this discussion in the following
corollary :

Corollary 5.1
.

Given the initial conditions in Section
4
, there exists a unique solution

(5.98)

(N,\beta,\tau,H,\gamma,\varphi,\omega)

to the reduced system (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) such that :

•

\gamma
and
N
admit the decompositions

\gamma=-\alpha\chi\ln+\tilde{\gamma},\qquad N=1+N_{a}\chi\ln+\tilde{N},

where
\alpha\geq 0
is a constant,
N_{a}(t)\geq 0
is a function of
t
alone and

\tilde{\gamma}\in H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}},\quad\mathbf{T}\tilde{\gamma}\in H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1},\quad\partial_{t}\mathbf{T}\tilde{\gamma}\in H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1},\quad\tilde{N}\in H^{4}_{\delta},\quad\partial_{t}\tilde{N}\in H^{2}_{\delta},

with estimates depending on
C_{i}
,
\delta
and
R
.

•

(\beta,\tau,H)
are in the following spaces :

\beta\in H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}},\quad e_{0}\beta\in H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}},\quad\tau\in H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1},\quad\partial_{t}\tau\in H^{1}_{\delta^{\prime}+1},\quad H,e_{0}H\in H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}+1},

with estimates depending on
C_{i}
,
\delta
and
R
.

•

The smallness conditions in (
5.8
) and (
5.11
) and Proposition
5.2
hold (without the
(n)
).

6.
End of the proof of Theorem
3.1

In this section we conclude the proof of Theorem
3.1
in two steps. As a first step, we show that the unique solution of the reduced system obtained in Corollary (
5.1
) is actually a solution of the full system (
2.2
). As we will see in Proposition
6.1
, this involves among other things propagating the gauge condition
\tau=0
(the condition
\bar{g}=e^{2\gamma}\delta
is also a gauge condition but we don’t need to propagate it). As in the harmonic gauge, this step is done using the Bianchi equation and the constraint equations. While in the harmonic gauge the Bianchi equation implies a second order hyperbolic system for the gauge, here we obtain a transport system (see Proposition
6.2
).

In a second step, we prove the remaining estimates stated in Theorem
3.1
, i.e the
H^{4}
norm of the metric coefficients with a loss of one regularity order for each time derivative. For this, we use the full Einstein equations in the elliptic gauge, thanks to the first step.

6.1.
Solving the Einstein equations

In order to solve (
2.2
) in the elliptic gauge, it only remains to prove that
G_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}
(the wave equations for
\varphi
and
\omega
being already included into the reduced system) and that
\tau=0
.

To define properly the tensors
G
and
T
we need to define a metric. Let
g
be the metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]
defined by the geometric quantities
N
,
\gamma
and
\beta
(obtained from (
5.98
)) as in (
2.9
). To compute the Einstein tensor of
g
, we need the second form fundamental and its traceless part. We define
K
with
H
,
\gamma
and
\tau
(obtained from (
5.98
)) according to (
2.6
) and (
2.7
). Thanks to (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) we have

\displaystyle K_{ij}=H_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\gamma}\tau\delta_{ij}=-\frac{1}{2N}e_{0}\left(e^{2\gamma}\right)\delta_{ij}+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2N}\left(\partial_{i}\beta_{j}+\partial_{j}\beta_{i}\right).

By (
A.6
), this proves that
K_{ij}
is the second fundamental form of
\Sigma_{t}
. On the other hand, by (
3.6
), we know that
\tau
is the mean curvature of
\Sigma_{t}
. This implies that
H_{ij}
is the traceless part of
K_{ij}
with respect to
\bar{g}=e^{2\gamma}\delta
. We also define the tensor
T
with
g
and
(\varphi,\omega)
(obtained from (
5.98
)) according to (
2.3
).

We can now use both our computations in the elliptic gauge and the reduced system to compute
G_{00}-T_{00}
and
G_{ij}-T_{ij}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Given a solution to (
3.4
)-(
3.10
), the Einstein tensor in the basis
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
is given by :

(6.1)

\displaystyle G_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}e_{0}\tau+T_{00},

(6.2)

\displaystyle G_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\gamma}e_{0}\tau}{2N}\delta_{ij}+T_{ij}.

Moreover, we have
D^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0
.

Proof.

In this proof we just have to put together our calculations about
R_{\mu\nu}
and
T_{\mu\nu}
perdormed in Appendix (
A
) and the reduced system (
3.4
)-(
3.10
). Note that putting (
3.6
) and (
3.8
) together gives back an elliptic equation satisfied by
\gamma
:

(6.3)

\Delta\gamma=\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2N}e_{0}\tau-\frac{\Delta N}{2N}-\left|\nabla\varphi\right|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2}.

In order to compute
G_{\mu\nu}
, we need the scalar curvature
R
, which, thanks to (
3.4
), (
6.3
) and (
A.13
), has the following expression :

R=-\mathbf{T}\tau+2e^{-2\gamma}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}-\frac{2}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\gamma-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}-\frac{1}{2N^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}(e_{0}\omega)^{2}.

We also recall the expression of
g_{\mu\nu}
in the
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
basis :
g_{00}=-N^{2}
,
g_{ij}=e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}
and
g_{0i}=0
.
Since
N
satisfies (
3.4
) and thanks to (
A.11
) we get :

\displaystyle G_{00}

\displaystyle=R_{00}-\frac{1}{2}g_{00}R

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}e_{0}\tau+(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}+N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left((e_{0}\omega)^{2}+e^{-2\gamma}N^{2}|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right),

which, looking at (
A.18
), gives (
6.1
). Thanks to (
3.7
) and (
A.9
) we get :

R_{ij}=\delta_{ij}\left(-\Delta\gamma+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\mathbf{T}\tau-\frac{\Delta N}{2N}\right)+2\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi-\delta_{ij}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{j}\omega-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\delta_{ij}|\nabla\omega|^{2},

which, using (
6.3
) gives
R_{ij}=2\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{j}\omega
. It gives us

G_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}\tau\delta_{ij}+2\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}\delta_{ij}-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\delta_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left(2\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{j}\omega+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\omega)^{2}\delta_{ij}-|\nabla\omega|^{2}\delta_{ij}\right),

which, looking at (
A.20
), gives (
6.2
). The conservation law
D^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0
is just a consequence of (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
A.25
).
∎

By Proposition
6.1
, in order to show that a solution to (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) is indeed a solution to (
2.2
) it remains
to show that
\tau=0
and
G_{0i}-T_{0i}=0
. These will be shown simultaneously and the Bianchi identities

D^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=0

are used in the following proposition to obtain a coupled system for this two quantities. For the sake of clarity, we use the following notations :

A_{i}\vcentcolon=G_{0i}-T_{0i},\qquad B_{i}\vcentcolon=G_{0i}-T_{0i}-\frac{N}{2}\partial_{i}\tau,

and
\mathrm{div}(A)=\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}A_{j}
. The important remark about these quantities is that if we manage to show that
e_{0}\tau=0
,
A_{i}=0
and
B_{i}=0
, we first have
G_{0i}-T_{0i}=0
, which, looking at the expression of
B_{i}
implies that
\nabla\tau=0
, which, in addition to
e_{0}\tau=0
and
\tau_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
implies that
\tau=0
in the whole space-time.

Proposition 6.2
.

The quantities
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
and
e_{0}\tau
satisfy the following coupled system :

(6.4)

\displaystyle e_{0}A_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}\partial_{i}e_{0}\tau+\frac{\partial_{i}N}{2}e_{0}\tau+\left(\mathbf{T}N+N\tau\right)A_{i}+\partial_{i}\beta^{j}A_{j},

(6.5)

\displaystyle e_{0}B_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{i}N}{2}e_{0}\tau+N\tau A_{i}+\mathbf{T}NB_{i}+\partial_{i}\beta^{j}B_{j},

(6.6)

\displaystyle e_{0}\left(e_{0}\tau\right)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\gamma}N\mathrm{div}(A)+2e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}NA_{j}+(2N\tau+\mathbf{T}N)e_{0}\tau.

Proof.

The equation (
6.5
) follows from (
6.4
) and we omit the proof, which is a direct computation. Thanks to the previous proposition and the Bianchi identity, we have
D^{\mu}(G_{\mu\nu}-T_{\mu\nu})=0
.

We first prove (
6.4
). By (
A.2
) and (
A.3
),

\displaystyle D_{0}(G_{0i}-T_{0i})

\displaystyle=e_{0}(G_{0i}-T_{0i})-\mathbf{T}N(G_{0i}-T_{0i})-e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{jk}N\partial_{k}N(G_{ij}-T_{ij})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial_{i}N}{N}(G_{00}-T_{00})-\frac{1}{2}\left(2\delta^{j}_{i}e_{0}\gamma+\partial_{i}\beta^{j}-\delta_{ik}\delta^{j\ell}\partial_{\ell}\beta^{k}\right)(G_{0j}-T_{0j})

(6.7)

\displaystyle=e_{0}(G_{0i}-T_{0i})-\mathbf{T}N(G_{0i}-T_{0i})-\partial_{i}Ne_{0}\tau

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\left(2\delta^{j}_{i}e_{0}\gamma+\partial_{i}\beta^{j}-\delta_{ik}\delta^{j\ell}\partial_{\ell}\beta^{k}\right)(G_{0j}-T_{0j}),

where in the last equality we have used (
6.1
) and (
6.2
). Similarly, by (
A.5
) and (
6.1
)-(
6.2
),

(6.8)

\displaystyle g^{jk}D_{j}(G_{ki}-T_{ki})=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\mathbf{T}\tau-\frac{\tau}{N}(G_{0i}-T_{0i})-\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\left(2\delta_{i}^{k}e_{0}\gamma-\delta_{\ell}^{k}\partial_{i}\beta^{\ell}-\delta_{i\ell}\delta^{jk}\partial_{j}\beta^{\ell}\right)(G_{0k}-T_{0k})

Thanks to
D^{\mu}(G_{\mu i}-T_{\mu i})=0
, we have

\text{\eqref{DoAoi}}-N^{2}\times\text{\eqref{DjAji}}=0,

which, after some straightforward simplifications, gives exactly (
6.4
).

We now prove (
6.6
). By (
A.2
) and (
6.1
),

(6.9)

D_{0}(G_{00}-T_{00})=\frac{N}{2}e_{0}(e_{0}\tau)-\frac{e_{0}N}{2}e_{0}\tau-2e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{ij}N\partial_{i}N(G_{0j}-T_{0j}).

On the other hand, by (
A.4
)-(
A.5
) and (
6.1
)-(
6.2
),

(6.10)

g^{ij}D_{i}(G_{j0}-T_{j0})=e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}(G_{j0}-T_{j0})-e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{ij}\frac{\partial_{i}N}{N}(G_{j0}-T_{j0})+\tau e_{0}\tau.

Thanks to
D^{\mu}(G_{\mu 0}-T_{\mu 0})=0
, we have

-\frac{1}{N}\times\text{\eqref{DoAoo}}+N\times\text{\eqref{DiAi0}}=0,

which, after some straightforward simplifications, gives exactly (
6.6
).
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose the solution to (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) as constructed in Section
5
arises from initial
data with
\tau_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
and that the constraint equations are initially satisfied, then the
solution satisfies

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle G_{0i}

\displaystyle=T_{0i}.

As a consequence, the solution to (
3.4
)-(
3.10
) is indeed a solution to (
2.2
).

Proof.

We set the following energy :

E(t)\vcentcolon=\left\|e_{0}\tau\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{i=1,2}\left(\left\|2e^{-\gamma}A_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|B_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).

We first note that
E(0)=0
because our solution arises from initial date satisfying the constraint equations (which implies that
(G_{0i}-T_{0i})_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
) and because
\tau_{|\Sigma_{0}}=0
. Our goal is to show that
E(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

We first multiply (
6.5
) by
B_{i}
and sum over
i=1,2
the two equations we obtain. We integrate over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and write
e_{0}=\partial_{t}-\beta\cdot\nabla
to obtain (after an integration by part on the last term) :

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{i=1,2}\left\|B_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{\partial_{i}N}{2}B_{i}e_{0}\tau+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}N\tau B_{i}A_{i}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\mathbf{T}NB_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\partial_{i}\beta^{j}B_{i}B_{j}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{div}(\beta)B_{i}^{2}.

Using Corollary
5.1
and Proposition
B.1
, we see that the quantities
\nabla N
,
N\tau
,
\mathbf{T}N
and
\nabla\beta
are bounded (for
N\tau
and
N\partial_{i}N
, we use the decay property of
\tau
and
\partial_{i}N
to deal with the logarithmic growth of
N
). Using the trick
2ab\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
and the fact that
1\lesssim e^{-\gamma}
, we get :

(6.11)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{i=1,2}\left\|B_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq CE(t).

Similarly, multiplying (
6.6
) by
e_{0}\tau
, we get :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|e_{0}\tau\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\gamma}N\,\mathrm{div}(A)e_{0}\tau+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\tau\partial_{i}NA_{i}+2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(2N\tau+\mathbf{T}N)\left(e_{0}\tau\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\beta)\left(e_{0}\tau\right)^{2}

(6.12)

\displaystyle=-4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-2\gamma}NA_{i}\partial_{i}e_{0}\tau+O(E(t)),

where we integrated by part the first term and bound the other terms just as we did for
\|B_{i}\|_{L^{2}}
, mainly using Corollary
5.1
. Now, writing
\partial_{t}=e_{0}+\beta\cdot\nabla
and integrating by part, we get :

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{i=1,2}\|e^{-\gamma}A_{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}e^{-2\gamma}A_{i}e_{0}A_{i}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\mathrm{div}\left(e^{-2\gamma}\beta\right)A_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}\partial_{t}(-2\tilde{\gamma})e^{-2\gamma}A_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}e^{-2\gamma}A_{i}e_{0}A_{i}+O(E(t)).

Using (
6.4
), we thus get :

(6.13)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\sum_{i=1,2}\|2e^{-\gamma}A_{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\sum_{i=1,2}e^{-2\gamma}NA_{i}\partial_{i}e_{0}\tau+O(E(t)).

Looking at (
6.12
) and (
6.13
), we see that our choice of scaling in the expression of
E(t)
implies a cancellation and we finally get, recalling (
6.11
) :

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}E(t)\leq CE(t)

which, using the Gronwall’s Lemma and
E(0)=0
, implies that
E(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
, which implies the desired result.
∎

6.2.
Improved regularity

To conclude the proof of the Theorem
3.1
, it only remains to prove the bounds stated in this theorem. Notice that some of the estimates are already obtained in Corollary
5.1
. This improvement of regularity is due to the fact that we now know that the solution of the reduced system is also a solution to the system (
2.2
), and therefore all the metric components solves elliptic equations.

Lemma 6.1
.

The metric components
N
,
\gamma
and
\beta
satisfy the following elliptic equations :

(6.14)

\displaystyle\Delta N

\displaystyle=e^{-2\gamma}N|H|^{2}+\frac{2e^{2\gamma}}{N}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}+\frac{e^{2\gamma-4\varphi}}{2N}(e_{0}\omega)^{2},

(6.15)

\displaystyle\Delta\gamma

\displaystyle=-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}|\nabla\omega|^{2}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}-\frac{e^{2\gamma-4\varphi}}{4N^{2}}(e_{0}\omega)^{2}-\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{2}|H|^{2},

(6.16)

\displaystyle\Delta\beta^{j}

\displaystyle=\delta^{jk}\delta^{i\ell}(L\beta)_{ik}\left(\frac{\partial_{\ell}N}{2N}-\partial_{\ell}\gamma\right)-2\delta^{kj}e_{0}\varphi\partial_{k}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\delta^{kj}e_{0}\omega\partial_{k}\omega.

Proof.

Since we solved (
2.2
), we have
R_{00}=T_{00}-g_{00}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T
, which, according to (
A.11
) and (
A.22
), easily implies (
6.14
).

Using (
A.11
), (
A.13
) and the fact that
\tau=0
, we get that

G_{00}=N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}\left(-\Delta\gamma-\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{2}|H|^{2}\right),

Using (
A.18
) and the fact that
G_{00}=T_{00}
we get (
6.15
).

The equation
R_{0j}=2e_{0}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\omega\partial_{j}\omega
and the fact that
\tau=0
together with (
A.10
) and (
A.7
) gives (
6.16
).

∎

In the following proposition, we state and prove the missing estimates :

Proposition 6.4
.

Taking
\varepsilon_{0}
smaller if necessary, the following estimates hold :

(6.17)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta}}+\left\|\beta\right\|_{H^{4}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

(6.18)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}\beta\right\|_{H^{3}_{\delta^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h},

(6.19)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}^{2}N_{a}\right|+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{N}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\beta\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta^{\prime}}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{\gamma}\right\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{h}.

Proof.

The idea is just to apply Corollary
B.1
to the equations (
6.14
)-(
6.15
)-(
6.16
), after having proven, using the regularity obtained in Corollary
5.1
, that the RHS of these equations are in the appropriate spaces. For the estimates involving time derivatives, we proceed in the same way after having differenciated once or twice the equations (
6.14
)-(
6.15
)-(
6.16
). We omit the details, since the computations are straightforward (mainly because now we don’t have to worry about the constants in the estimates).

∎

This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.1
.

7.
Proof of Theorem
3.2

7.1.
Almost
H^{2}
well-posedness

At this stage, thanks to Theorem
3.1
, we proved that the system (
2.2
) is well posed locally in time with initial data
(\partial\varphi,\partial\omega)\in H^{2}
. The next step would be to consider initial data
(\partial\varphi,\partial\omega)
which are only in
H^{1}
. In order to obtain well-posedness in this setting, we could regularize the initial data with a sequence
(\partial\varphi_{n},\partial\omega_{n})\in H^{2}
to which we can apply Theorem
3.1
, thus obtaining a sequence of solution to (
2.2
) on
[0,T_{n})
. A priori, if
(\partial\varphi,\partial\omega)
only belongs to
H^{1}
, the
H^{2}
norm of
(\partial\varphi_{n},\partial\omega_{n})
explodes as
n
tends to
+\infty
and therefore the sequence
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to 0, forbidding us to define a limit on some non-trivial interval.

To prevent this to happen, we need to prove that the
H^{2}
and
L^{4}
estimates of each
(\partial\varphi_{n},\partial\omega_{n})
can be propagated on some fixed interval using only their
H^{1}
norm (which are bounded by the
H^{1}
norm of the initial data) using the system that
\varphi
and
\omega
solve, i.e the system (
7.3
)-(
7.4
) below. As we will see in the rest of this section, it is possible to improve the
H^{2}
norm. But unfortunately, we can’t improve the
L^{4}
estimates using only the
H^{1}
norm and the system (
7.3
)-(
7.4
). Note that this difficulty already occured in the proof of Theorem
3.1
but we bypassed it by taking advantage of the smallness of the time of existence (see Proposition
5.2
), something that we cannot do in this approximation procedure.

Therefore, we can’t prove local well-posedness at the
H^{2}
level. Instead we prove a blow-up criterium, meaning that the
L^{4}
estimates that we can’t propagate is assumed to hold from the start. It only remains to improve the
H^{2}
estimates.

7.2.
The wave map structure

To prove Theorem
3.2
, we argue by contradiction and assume throughout this section that the following statements both hold on
[0,T)
:

(7.1)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial\omega\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0},

(7.2)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\varphi\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{0},

for some
C_{0}>0
, and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
defined in Theorem
3.1
, and where
T
is the maximal time of existence of a solution to (
2.2
). The goal is to show that we can actually bound the
H^{2}
norm of
\partial\varphi
and
\partial\omega
on
[0,T)
, and hence up to
T
, using (
7.1
). Then, using in addition (
7.2
) and applying Theorem
3.1
, we construct a solution of (
2.2
) beyond the time
T
. This would contradict the maximality of
T
, and thus prove Theorem
3.2
.

In order to estimate the
H^{2}
norm of
\partial\varphi
and
\partial\omega
using (
7.1
), we are going to use the wave map structure of the coupled wave equations solved by
\varphi
and
\omega
, which we recall :

(7.3)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\varphi

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\omega,

(7.4)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\omega

\displaystyle=4\partial^{\rho}\omega\partial_{\rho}\varphi.

We also recall the expression of the operator
\Box_{g}
in the case
\tau=0
:

(7.5)

\Box_{g}f=-\mathbf{T}^{2}f+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla f),

where
f
is any function on
\mathcal{M}
. Note the following notation for the rest of this section :
U
stands for
\varphi
or
\omega
,
g
stands for any metric coefficient, meaning
N
,
\gamma
and
\beta
.

7.2.1.
The naive energy estimate

We want to control the
H^{2}
norm of
\partial U
. As
U
satisfies a wave equation, we could use Lemma
5.4
. With our formal notation, this wave equation writes

\Box_{g}U=g^{-1}(\partial U)^{2}.

Thus, Lemma
5.4
would basically implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\partial\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{high}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial\nabla^{2}U\nabla^{2}\left(g^{-1}(\partial U)^{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{high}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|(\partial\nabla^{2}U)^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{1}}+\cdots,

where the dots reprensent term easily bounded by
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}
. The problem is that, using only (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), the term
\left\|(\partial\nabla^{2}U)^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{1}}
cannot be bounded by
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}
, it requires necessarily
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2+\eta}
with
\eta>0
. Thus, a continuity argument, aiming at proving boundedness in
H^{2}
, would be impossible to carry out.

Therefore, we need to use deeper the structure of the coupled equations (
7.3
) and (
7.4
). This structure will allows us to define a third order energy, which will have the property of avoiding
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2+\eta}
terms into the energy estimate.

7.2.2.
The third order energy

The system (
7.3
)-(
7.4
) has actually more structure than we could expect : it is a wave map system, as shown in
[
10
]
. More precisely, if we consider the map
u=(\varphi,\omega)
, then
u
is an harmonic map from
([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3},g)
to
(\mathbb{R}^{2},h)
with
h
being the following metric :

2(\mathrm{d}x)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4x}(\mathrm{d}y)^{2}.

For those wave map systems, Choquet-Bruhat in
[
2
]
noted that we can define a third order energy, which in our case is

\mathscr{E}_{3}\vcentcolon=\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}+\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega},

with

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2\left[\frac{1}{N^{2}}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)^{2}+e^{-2\gamma}\left|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\left[\frac{1}{N^{2}}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+e^{-2\gamma}\left|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}x.

Our goal is to show that we can estimate
\mathscr{E}_{3}
by
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}
. We start by commuting
\Box_{g}
with
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}
to obtain :

(7.6)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+e^{-4\varphi}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega

\displaystyle=F^{\varphi}_{ij},

(7.7)

\displaystyle\Box_{g}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-4g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{\beta}\varphi-4g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi

\displaystyle=F^{\omega}_{ij},

where we set

\displaystyle F^{\varphi}_{ij}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left[\Box_{g},\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\right]\varphi+-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\left(e^{-4\varphi}g^{\alpha\beta}\right)\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega

(7.8)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\partial_{(i}\left(e^{-4\varphi}g^{\alpha\beta}\right)\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{j)}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega-e^{-4\varphi}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{\beta}\partial_{j}\omega,

\displaystyle F^{\omega}_{ij}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left[\Box_{g},\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\right]\omega+4\partial_{i}\partial_{j}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\varphi

(7.9)

\displaystyle\qquad+4\partial_{(i}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\partial_{j)}\varphi+4\partial_{(i}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{j)}\omega\partial_{\beta}\varphi+4g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{(i}\omega\partial_{\beta}\partial_{j)}\varphi.

We also define the following quantity :

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\|\partial_{t}\gamma\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\left\|\partial\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|\partial U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

(7.10)

\displaystyle\quad\;+\left(\left\|\partial\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}U(\partial U)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|(\partial U)^{3}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|F^{U}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\left\|(\partial\nabla U)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla g\nabla^{3}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla g\nabla U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\right)

where by
F^{U}
we mean either
F^{\varphi}
or
F^{\omega}
. For clarity, the computations for the time derivative of the energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}
are done in Appendix
C
, where we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 7.1
.

The energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}
satisfies

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}=O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

This proposition shows the interest of the energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}
: its time derivative do not include terms of the form
\left\|(\partial\nabla^{2}U)^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{1}}
, unlike the usual energy estimate of Lemma
5.4
.

7.3.
Continuity argument

Before starting the continuity argument, we need to show that
\mathscr{R}
can be bounded by
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}
(Lemmas
7.1
and
7.2
) and to compare
\mathscr{E}_{3}
with
\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}
(Lemma
7.3
). To this end, we will use the following key estimates :

(7.11)

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|u\right\|_{H^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(7.12)

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\nabla^{2}u\right\|_{L^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Both are consequences of the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality, see Proposition
B.5
. We will use without mention the fact that
\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\omega\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{4}}+\left\|\omega\right\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
(since
\varphi
and
\omega
are compactly supported function and because of (
7.2
)), and also the fact that
\left\|g\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
. We also need to estimate
\nabla^{3}g
. For this, we apply the usual elliptic estimate to the equation
\Delta g=(\nabla g)^{2}+(\partial U)^{2}
(this is the type of equations solved by the metric coefficients in the elliptic gauge, see Lemma
6.1
). It first gives :

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{W^{2,\frac{4}{3}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}g\nabla g\right\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}}+\left\|\partial\nabla U\partial U\right\|_{L^{\frac{4}{3}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

where we used Hölder’s inequality
L^{2}\times L^{4}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\frac{4}{3}}
and the embedding
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
. The embedding
W^{2,\frac{4}{3}}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
then gives :

(7.13)

\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}+C_{0}^{2}.

The
L^{2}
elliptic estimate implies :

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}g\nabla g\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial\nabla U\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{H^{2}}+\varepsilon_{0}C_{0}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

where we used
\left\|g\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
, the Hölder’s inequality and (
7.11
). Taking
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough this gives :

(7.14)

\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In the sequel, we will commute without mention
\partial
and
\nabla
since
[e_{0},\nabla]=\nabla\beta\nabla
and
\nabla\beta
can be bounded using (
7.13
).

7.3.1.
The energy
\mathscr{R}

We start by the estimates for
F^{U}
:

Lemma 7.1
.

There exists
C(C_{0})>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|F^{\varphi}_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|F^{\omega}_{ij}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

Proof.

The expressions of
F^{\varphi}_{ij}
and
F^{\omega}_{ij}
are given by (
7.8
) and (
7.9
). We start by estimate the commutator
[\Box_{g},\nabla^{2}]U
. Looking at the expression (
7.5
), we start by the spatial part of
\Box_{g}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla^{2},g\nabla(g\nabla\cdot)\right]U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|g\nabla^{3}g\nabla U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla g\nabla^{2}g\nabla U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|g\nabla^{2}g\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|(\nabla g)^{2}\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|g\nabla g\nabla^{3}U\right\|_{L^{2}}.

For the last two terms, we simply bound
\nabla g
using (
7.13
) and put
\partial U
in
H^{2}
:

\displaystyle\left\|(\nabla g)^{2}\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|g\nabla g\nabla^{3}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left(\left\|\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{3}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\right).

We do the same for the second term, using in addition
\left\|\nabla^{2}g\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1
and the embedding
H^{2}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\nabla^{2}g\nabla U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\nabla U\right\|_{L^{\infty}}.

We deal with the third term using first the Hölder’s inequality, the embedding
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
, (
7.14
) and (
7.11
) :

\displaystyle\left\|g\nabla^{2}g\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\left\|\nabla^{2}g\right\|_{H^{1}}\left\|\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{H^{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

For the first term, we put
\nabla^{3}g
in
L^{2}
and
\nabla U
in
L^{\infty}
, and then use (
7.14
) and (
7.12
) :

\displaystyle\left\|g\nabla^{3}g\nabla U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left\|\nabla U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Summarizing everything we obtain :

(7.15)

\left\|\left[\nabla^{2},g\nabla(g\nabla\cdot)\right]U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

We now estimate the contribution of
\mathbf{T}^{2}
to the commutator. We have :

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla^{2},\mathbf{T}^{2}\right]U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}g\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla g\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial_{t}g\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{2}g\nabla\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla g\nabla^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}

The last two terms have already been estimated during the proof of (
7.15
). For the first two terms, we use the equation
\Box_{g}U=(\partial U)^{2}
satisfied by
U
to express
\partial_{t}^{2}U
. It shows that

(7.16)

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}^{2}U|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+|\nabla g|\right)|\partial U|^{2}+|g\nabla^{2}U|\lesssim C(C_{0})\left(|\partial U|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}U|\right)

where we also used (
7.13
). We can put
\nabla^{2}g
in
L^{2}
and
\partial U
in
L^{\infty}
using (
7.12
) (note that the second term has already been estimated) :

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}g\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}g(\partial U)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla^{2}g\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}

The equation
\Box_{g}U=(\partial U)^{2}
also gives us

|\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U|\lesssim|\nabla\partial U\partial U|+|\nabla^{2}g\nabla U|+|\nabla^{3}U|+|\nabla g\nabla^{2}U|.

Because of (
7.13
),
\left\|\nabla g\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}
and the previous estimate shows therefore that all the terms in
\left\|\nabla g\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}
have already been estimated. This gives :

\left\|\nabla g\nabla\partial_{t}^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

It remains to deal with the term involving
\partial_{t}g
. This quantity satisfies the following equation :

\Delta\partial_{t}g=\nabla\partial_{t}g\nabla g+\partial_{t}^{2}U\nabla U+\partial_{t}U\nabla\partial_{t}U.

The usual elliptic estimates gives us

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}g\right\|_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla\partial_{t}g\nabla g\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}U\nabla U\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\partial_{t}U\nabla\partial_{t}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\left\|\nabla\partial_{t}g\right\|_{H^{1}}+C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}

where we used (
7.11
) and (
7.12
). Taking
\varepsilon_{0}
small enough, this shows that
\left\|\partial_{t}g\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
. With this, we estimate the remaining term in the commutator
\left[\nabla^{2},\mathbf{T}^{2}\right]
using in addition (
7.12
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial_{t}g\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial_{t}g\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}

Thus, we obtain :

(7.17)

\left\|\left[\nabla^{2},\mathbf{T}^{2}\right]U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

Putting (
7.15
) and (
7.17
) together we finally obtain :

\left\|\left[\Box_{g},\nabla^{2}\right]U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

The lemma is actually proved because all the remaining terms in
F^{U}
have already been estimated in the proof of (
7.15
) and (
7.17
).
∎

We now estimate
\mathscr{R}
:

Lemma 7.2
.

There exists
C^{\prime}(C_{0})>0
such that

\mathscr{R}\leq C^{\prime}(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

First, note that the previous lemma handles the term
\left\|F^{U}\right\|_{L^{2}}
. Most of the remaining terms in
\mathscr{R}
can simply be estimated using the Hölder’s inequality, (
7.11
) and (
7.12
) :

\displaystyle\left\|\partial U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}U(\partial U)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}C_{0}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|(\partial U)^{3}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|(\partial\nabla U)^{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{0}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\nabla U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

Let us give the details only for the last one. We bound
\nabla g
with (
7.13
), and then we the Hölder’s inequality
L^{4}\times L^{4}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{2}
and the embedding
H^{1}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{4}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\nabla U\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\nabla g\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|\nabla U\right\|_{L^{4}}\left\|\nabla^{2}U\right\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}C(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

Because of (
A.8
) and the gauge condition
\tau=0
we have
|\partial_{t}\gamma|\lesssim|\nabla g|
so we estimate
\left\|\partial_{t}\gamma\right\|_{L^{\infty}}
with (
7.13
). Samewise with (
7.13
) we estimate the very last term appearing in
\mathscr{R}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla g\nabla^{3}U\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim(\varepsilon_{0}^{2}+C_{0}^{2})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}.

∎

In the next lemma, we compare
\mathscr{E}_{3}
with the
H^{2}
norm of
\partial U
. We omit the proof since all the terms involved have been already estimated in the two previous lemmas.

Lemma 7.3
.

There exists
K(C_{0})>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{3}

\displaystyle\leq K(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq K(C_{0})\mathscr{E}_{3}+\varepsilon_{0}^{2}K(C_{0})\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}.

7.3.2.
Conclusion

Putting everything together, we can now complete the continuity argument by propagating the
H^{2}
regularity. We consider the following bootstrap assumption :

(7.18)

\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}(t)\leq C_{1}\exp(C_{1}t),

with
C_{1}>0
to be chosen later. Let
T_{0}<T
be the maximal time such that (
7.18
) holds for all
0\leq t\leq T_{0}
. Note that if
C_{1}
is large enough we have
T_{0}>0
, since
\partial\varphi
and
\partial\omega
are initially in
H^{2}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

If
\varepsilon_{0}
is small enough (still independent of
C_{high}
) and
C_{1}
is large enough, the following holds on
[0,T_{0}]
:

(7.19)

\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}(t)\leq\frac{1}{2}C_{1}\exp(C_{1}t).

Proof.

The
H^{1}
norm of
\partial U
is already controled, so it suffices to prove the bound stated in the proposition for
\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}
. For this, we use the Proposition
7.1
, which implies that for
t\in[0,T_{0}]
(we also use Lemma
7.3
and (
7.18
)) :

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq K^{2}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}(0)+\varepsilon_{0}^{2}K\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}(t)+CK\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{R}(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq K^{2}C_{high}^{2}+\varepsilon_{0}^{2}KC_{1}^{2}\exp(2C_{1}t)+CK\int_{0}^{t}\mathscr{R}(s)\mathrm{d}s,

for some
C>0
given by Proposition
7.1
. We now use Lemma
7.2
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}\partial U\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(t)

\displaystyle\leq K^{2}C_{high}^{2}+\varepsilon_{0}^{2}KC_{1}^{2}\exp(2C_{1}t)+CKC^{\prime}(C_{0})\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial U\right\|_{H^{2}}^{2}(s)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq K^{2}C_{high}^{2}+\varepsilon_{0}^{2}KC_{1}^{2}\exp(2C_{1}t)+\frac{1}{2}CKC^{\prime}(C_{0})C_{1}\exp(2C_{1}t).

We now choose
C_{1}\geq\max\left(3CKC^{\prime}(C_{0}),\sqrt{6}KC_{high}\right)
and
\varepsilon_{0}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{6K}}
, so that each term of the previous inequality is bounded by
\frac{1}{6}C_{1}^{2}\exp(2C_{1}t)
. This concludes the proof.

∎

By continuity of the quantities involved, the previous proposition contradicts the maximality of
T_{0}
, and thus proves that
T_{0}=T
. As explained at the beginning of Section
7
, this concludes the proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Appendix A
Computations in the elliptic gauge

In this section, we collect some computations for the spacetime metric in the elliptic gauge defined in Section
2.3
. See also
[
8
]
.

A.1.
Connection coefficients

The 2+1 metric
g
has the form

g=-N^{2}\mathrm{d}t+\bar{g}_{ij}\left(\mathrm{d}x^{i}+\beta^{i}\mathrm{d}t\right)\left(\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\beta^{j}\mathrm{d}t\right),

with
\bar{g}=e^{2\gamma}\delta
.
In the basis
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
, we have
g_{00}=-N^{2}
,
g_{0i}=0
and
g_{ij}=e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}
, which gives
\det(g)=-e^{4\gamma}N^{2}
.

In the basis
(\partial_{t},\partial_{i})
we have :

(A.1)

g^{-1}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\begin{pmatrix}-1&\beta^{1}&\beta^{2}\\
\beta^{1}&N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}-\left(\beta^{1}\right)^{2}&-\beta^{1}\beta^{2}\\
\beta^{2}&-\beta^{1}\beta^{2}&N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}-\left(\beta^{2}\right)^{2}\end{pmatrix}

This allows us to compute
\Box_{g}h
for
h
a function on
\mathcal{M}
:

Proposition A.1
.

If
h
is a function on
\mathcal{M}
, we have

\displaystyle\Box_{g}h

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{T}^{2}h+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla h)+\tau\mathbf{T}h

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{T}^{2}h+e^{-2\gamma}\Delta h+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\nabla h\cdot\nabla N+\tau\mathbf{T}h.

Proof.

By definition of
\Box_{g}
, we have :

\displaystyle\Box_{g}h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\det(g)|}}\partial_{\beta}\left(g^{\beta\alpha}\sqrt{|\det(g)|}\partial_{\alpha}h\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N}\left(-\partial_{t}h+\beta^{1}\partial_{1}h+\beta^{2}\partial_{2}h\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\partial_{1}\left(\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N}\left(\beta^{1}\partial_{t}h+\left(N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}-\left(\beta^{1}\right)^{2}\right)\partial_{1}h-\beta^{1}\beta^{2}\partial_{2}h\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\partial_{2}\left(\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N}\left(\beta^{2}\partial_{t}h-\beta^{1}\beta^{2}\partial_{1}h+\left(N^{2}e^{-2\gamma}-\left(\beta^{2}\right)^{2}\right)\partial_{2}h\right)\right),

where we used the expression of
g^{-1}
in the basis
(\partial_{t},\partial_{i})
(see expression (
A.1
)). By rearranging the terms, we get :

\displaystyle\Box_{g}h

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N}\partial_{t}\mathbf{T}h-\frac{2\partial_{t}\gamma}{N}\mathbf{T}h+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}\left(e^{2\gamma}\mathbf{T}h\beta+N\nabla h\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{T}^{2}h+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla h)+\left(-2\mathbf{T}\gamma+\frac{\mathrm{div}(\beta)}{N}\right)\mathbf{T}h.

This proves the proposition, by looking at (
A.8
).
∎

We now compute the connection coefficients for the metric (
2.9
) in the basis
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
. Notice that
[e_{0},\partial_{i}]=\partial_{i}\beta^{j}\partial_{j}
. Using this, we compute :

\displaystyle g(D_{0}e_{0},e_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e_{0}g_{00}=-\frac{1}{2}e_{0}(N^{2})=-Ne_{0}N,

\displaystyle g(D_{i}e_{0},e_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}g_{00}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}(N^{2})=-N\partial_{i}N,

\displaystyle g(D_{0}e_{0},\partial_{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}g_{00}-g(e_{0},[e_{0},\partial_{i}])=N\partial_{i}N,

\displaystyle g(D_{i}e_{0},\partial_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(e_{0}g_{ij}-g(\partial_{i},[e_{0},\partial_{j}])-g(\partial_{j},[e_{0},\partial_{i}])\right)=\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\left(2e_{0}\gamma\delta_{ij}-\partial_{i}\beta^{k}\delta_{jk}-\partial_{j}\beta^{k}\delta_{ik}\right),

\displaystyle g(D_{0}\partial_{i},e_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{i}g_{00}+g(e_{0},[e_{0},\partial_{i}])=-N\partial_{i}N,

\displaystyle g(D_{0}\partial_{i},\partial_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(e_{0}g_{ij}+g(\partial_{i},[\partial_{j},e_{0}])+g(\partial_{j},[e_{0},\partial_{i}])\right)=\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\left(2e_{0}\gamma\delta_{ij}+\partial_{i}\beta^{k}\delta_{jk}-\partial_{j}\beta^{k}\delta_{ik}\right),

\displaystyle g(D_{i}\partial_{j},e_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(-e_{0}g_{ij}-g(\partial_{i},[\partial_{j},e_{0}])+g(\partial_{j},[e_{0},\partial_{i}])\right)=-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\left(2e_{0}\gamma\delta_{ij}-\partial_{i}\beta^{k}\delta_{jk}-\partial_{j}\beta^{k}\delta_{ik}\right),

\displaystyle g(D_{i}\partial_{j},\partial_{k})

\displaystyle=e^{2\gamma}\left(\delta_{ik}\partial_{j}\gamma+\delta_{jk}\partial_{i}\gamma-\delta_{ij}\delta^{\ell}_{k}\partial_{\ell}\gamma\right).

The first two expressions are derived using
X(g(Y,Z))=g(D_{X}Y,Z)+g(Y,D_{X}Z)
and the other ones with the Koszul formula :

2g(D_{V}W,X)=Vg(W,X)+Wg(X,V)-Xg(V,W)-g(V,[W,X])+g(W,[X,V])+g(X,[V,W]).

From the above calculations, we obtain

(A.2)

\displaystyle D_{0}e_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}Ne_{0}+e^{-2\gamma}\delta^{ij}N\partial_{i}N\partial_{j},

(A.3)

\displaystyle D_{0}\partial_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}N\mathbf{T}+\frac{1}{2}\left(2\delta^{j}_{i}e_{0}\gamma+\partial_{i}\beta^{j}-\delta_{ik}\delta^{j\ell}\partial_{\ell}\beta^{k}\right)\partial_{j},

(A.4)

\displaystyle D_{i}e_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}N\mathbf{T}+\frac{1}{2}\left(2\delta^{j}_{i}e_{0}\gamma-\partial_{i}\beta^{j}-\delta_{ik}\delta^{j\ell}\partial_{\ell}\beta^{k}\right)\partial_{j},

(A.5)

\displaystyle D_{i}\partial_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2N}\left(2\delta_{ij}e_{0}\gamma-\left(\partial_{i}\beta^{k}\right)\delta_{jk}-\left(\partial_{j}\beta^{k}\right)\delta_{ik}\right)\mathbf{T}+\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\partial_{j}\gamma+\delta^{k}_{j}\partial_{i}\gamma-\delta_{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{\ell}\gamma\right)\partial_{k}.

A.2.
Decomposition of the Ricci tensor

Proposition A.2
.

Given
g
of the form (
2.9
), we have the following identities :

(A.6)

\displaystyle K_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta_{ij}}{2}\mathbf{T}\left(e^{2\gamma}\right)+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2N}\left(\partial_{i}\beta_{j}+\partial_{j}\beta_{i}\right),

(A.7)

\displaystyle H_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2N}(L\beta)_{ij},

(A.8)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=-2\mathbf{T}\gamma+\frac{\mathrm{div}\left(\beta\right)}{N}.

Proof.

The equation (
A.6
) follows from (
2.5
), and (
A.7
) and (
A.8
) follow from (
A.6
).
∎

Proposition A.3
.

Given
g
of the form (
2.9
), the components of the Ricci tensor in the basis
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
are given by

(A.9)

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\left(-\Delta\gamma+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\mathbf{T}\tau-\frac{\Delta N}{2N}\right)-\mathbf{T}H_{ij}-2e^{-2\gamma}H_{i}^{\;\ell}H_{j\ell}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\left(\partial_{j}\beta^{k}H_{ki}+\partial_{i}\beta^{k}H_{kj}\right)-\frac{1}{N}\left(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}N-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\Delta N-\left(\delta_{i}^{k}\partial_{j}\gamma+\delta_{j}^{k}\partial_{i}\gamma-\delta_{ij}\delta^{\ell k}\partial_{\ell}\gamma\right)\partial_{k}N\right),

(A.10)

\displaystyle R_{0j}

\displaystyle=N\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{j}\tau-e^{-2\gamma}\partial^{i}H_{ij}\right),

(A.11)

\displaystyle R_{00}

\displaystyle=N\left(e_{0}\tau-e^{-4\gamma}N\left|H\right|^{2}-\frac{N\tau^{2}}{2}+e^{-2\gamma}\Delta N\right).

Moreover,

(A.12)

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}R_{ij}

\displaystyle=2\left(-\Delta\gamma+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}e^{2\gamma}-\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{2}\mathbf{T}\tau-\frac{\Delta N}{2N}\right),

(A.13)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=-2\mathbf{T}\tau+\frac{3}{2}\tau^{2}+e^{-4\gamma}\left|H\right|^{2}-2e^{-2\gamma}\frac{\Delta N}{N}-2e^{-2\gamma}\Delta\gamma.

Proof.

From Chapter 6 of
[
3
]
, we have

(A.14)

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle=\bar{R}_{ij}+K_{ij}\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}K-2K_{i}^{\;\ell}K_{j\ell}-N^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}K_{ij}+D_{i}\partial_{j}N\right),

(A.15)

\displaystyle R_{0j}

\displaystyle=N\left(\partial_{j}(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}K)-D_{\ell}K^{\ell}_{\;j}\right),

(A.16)

\displaystyle R_{00}

\displaystyle=N\left(e_{0}(\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}K)-N|K|^{2}+\Delta_{\bar{g}}N\right),

where
\bar{D}
,
\bar{R}_{ij}
and
\Delta_{\bar{g}}
are defined with respect to
\bar{g}
. First, by (
2.6
) and the connection coefficients computations, (
A.14
) becomes

(A.17)

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\delta_{ij}\Delta\gamma+\tau\left(H_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}\tau\right)-2e^{-2\gamma}\left(H_{i}^{\;\ell}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\gamma}\delta_{i}^{\ell}\tau\right)\left(H_{j\ell}+\frac{1}{2}e^{2\gamma}\delta_{j\ell}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{N}\left(\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}K_{ij}+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}N-\left(\delta_{i}^{k}\partial_{j}\gamma+\delta_{j}^{k}\partial_{i}\gamma-\delta_{ij}\delta^{k\ell}\partial_{\ell}\gamma\right)\partial_{k}N\right).

To proceed, we compute
\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}K_{ij}
by considering
H_{ij}
and
\tau
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}H_{ij}

\displaystyle=e_{0}H_{ij}-\partial_{j}\beta^{k}H_{ki}-\partial_{i}\beta^{k}H_{kj},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}(\tau\bar{g}_{ij})

\displaystyle=e^{2\gamma}\delta_{ij}e_{0}\tau-2N\tau K_{ij}.

Therefore, using (
2.6
) and plugging
\mathcal{L}_{e_{0}}K_{ij}
into (
A.17
), we obtain (
A.9
).

The expression of
R_{0j}
in (
A.10
) follows from (
A.15
) and the fact that for any covariant symmetric 2-tensor
A_{i}j
,

\bar{g}^{ik}\bar{D}_{k}A_{ij}=e^{-2\gamma}\partial^{i}A_{i}j-\partial_{j}\gamma\mathrm{tr}_{\bar{g}}A.

Using (
A.16
) and the conformal invariance of the Laplacian we easily get (
A.11
).

To prove (
A.12
), we first note that

\delta^{ij}(\partial_{j}\beta^{k}H_{ki}+\partial_{i}\beta^{k}H_{kj})=H_{ij}(L\beta)^{ij}.

Combining this with (
A.7
), we obtain

\delta^{ij}\left(-2e^{-2\gamma}H_{i}^{\;\ell}H_{j\ell}+\frac{1}{N}(\partial_{j}\beta^{k}H_{ki}+\partial_{i}\beta^{k}H_{kj})\right)=0.

Taking the trace of (
A.9
) and using this identity yield (
A.12
).

Finally, by putting (
2.9
), (
A.11
) and (
A.12
) we easily get (
A.13
).
∎

A.3.
The stress-energy-momentum tensor

Define
T_{\mu\nu}
by

T_{\mu\nu}=2\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\left(2\partial_{\mu}\omega\partial_{\nu}\omega-g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega\right).

Proposition A.4
.

The following identities are satisfied (with respect to the
(e_{0},\partial_{i})
basis) :

(A.18)

\displaystyle T_{00}

\displaystyle=(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}+e^{-2\gamma}N^{2}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left((e_{0}\omega)^{2}+e^{-2\gamma}N^{2}|\nabla\omega|^{2}\right),

(A.19)

\displaystyle T_{0j}

\displaystyle=2e_{0}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\omega\partial_{j}\omega,

(A.20)

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\varphi)^{2}\delta_{ij}-|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\delta_{ij}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}\left(2\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{j}\omega+\frac{e^{2\gamma}}{N^{2}}(e_{0}\omega)^{2}\delta_{ij}-|\nabla\omega|^{2}\delta_{ij}\right),

(A.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{g}T

\displaystyle=-g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\varphi\partial_{\beta}\varphi-\frac{1}{4}e^{-4\varphi}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega,

(A.22)

\displaystyle T_{00}-g_{00}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T

\displaystyle=2\left(e_{0}\varphi\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}(e_{0}\omega)^{2},

(A.23)

\displaystyle T_{ij}-g_{ij}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T

\displaystyle=2\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{i}\omega\partial_{j}\omega,

(A.24)

\displaystyle\delta^{ij}\left(T_{ij}-g_{ij}\mathrm{tr}_{g}T\right)

\displaystyle=2\left|\nabla\varphi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\left|\nabla\omega\right|^{2},

(A.25)

\displaystyle D^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=2(\Box_{g}\varphi)\partial_{\nu}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}(\Box_{g}\omega)\partial_{\nu}\omega

\displaystyle\qquad-e^{-4\varphi}\partial^{\mu}\varphi\left(2\partial_{\mu}\omega\partial_{\nu}\omega-g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\omega\partial_{\beta}\omega\right).

Appendix B
Weighted Sobolev spaces

Here are some results about weighted Sobolev spaces on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, which are systematically used during the proof. Most of them can be found in the Appendix I of
[
3
]
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
m\geq 1
,
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{W^{m-1,p}_{\delta+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{W^{m,p}_{\delta}},

\displaystyle\|\nabla u\|_{C^{m-1}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{C^{m}_{\delta+1}}.

We have an easy embedding result, which is a straightforward application of the Hölder’s inequality :

Lemma B.2
.

If
1\leq p_{1}\leq p_{2}\leq\infty
and
\delta_{2}-\delta_{1}>2\left(\frac{1}{p_{1}}-\frac{1}{p_{2}}\right)
, then we have the continuous embedding

L^{p_{2}}_{\delta_{2}}\xhookrightarrow{}L^{p_{1}}_{\delta_{1}}.

Next, we have Sobolev embedding theorems for weighted Sobolev spaces :

Proposition B.1
.

Let
s,m\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
1<p<\infty
.

•

If
s>\frac{2}{p}
and
\beta\leq\delta+\frac{2}{p}
, then we have the continuous embedding

W^{s+m,p}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}C^{m}_{\beta}.

•

If
s<\frac{2}{p}
, then we have the continuous embedding

W^{s+m,p}_{\delta}\xhookrightarrow{}W^{m,\frac{2p}{2-sp}}_{\delta+s}.

We will also need a product estimate.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
s,s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
,
\delta,\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s\leq\min(s_{1},s_{2})
,
s<s_{1}+s_{2}-\frac{2}{p}
and
\delta<\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\frac{2}{p}
. Then we have the continuous multiplication property

W^{s_{1},p}_{\delta_{1}}\times W^{s_{2},p}_{\delta_{2}}\xhookrightarrow{}W^{s,p}_{\delta}.

The following simple lemma will be useful as well.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
g\in L^{\infty}_{loc}
such that
|g(x)|\lesssim\langle x\rangle^{\alpha}
. Then the multiplication by
g
map
L^{2}_{\delta+\alpha}
to
L^{2}_{\delta}
with operator norm bounded by
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|g(x)|}{\langle x\rangle^{\alpha}}
.

The next result, which is due to McOwen, concerns the invertibility of the Laplacian on weighted Sobolev
spaces. Its proof can be found in
[
9
]
.

Theorem B.1
.

Let
m,s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
-1+m<\delta<m
. The Laplace operator
\Delta:H^{s+2}_{\delta}\longrightarrow H^{s}_{\delta+2}
is an injection with closed range

\left\{f\in H^{s}_{\delta+2}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \forall v\in\cup_{i=0}^{m}\mathcal{H}_{i},\;\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}fv=0\right\},

where
\mathcal{H}_{i}
is the set of harmonic polynomials of degree
i
. Moreover,
u
obeys the estimate

\|u\|_{H^{s+2}_{\delta}}\leq C(\delta,m,p)\|\Delta u\|_{H^{s}_{\delta+2}}.

The following is a corollary of Theorem
B.1
:

Corollary B.1
.

Let
-1<\delta<0
and
f\in H^{0}_{\delta+2}
. Then there exists a solution u of

\Delta u=f,

which can be written

u=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f\right)\chi(|x|)\ln(|x|)+v,

where
\chi
is as in Section
3.1
and
\|v\|_{H^{2}_{\delta}}\leq C(\delta)\|f\|_{H^{0}_{\delta+2}}
.

We will also use some classical inequalities, which we recall here, even if they are not related to weighted Sobolev spaces. The proof of the next property can be found in Appendix A of
[
12
]
.

Proposition B.3
.

If
s\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\|uv\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\|u\|_{H^{s}}\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|v\|_{H^{s}}\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}.

We recall the Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality :

Proposition B.4
.

If
0<\alpha<2
and
1<p<r<\infty
and
\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, then

\left\|u*\frac{1}{|\cdot|^{2-\alpha}}\right\|_{L^{r}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{p}}.

We recall the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality, for which a proof can be found in
[
5
]
:

Proposition B.5
.

Let
1\leq q,r\leq+\infty
,
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{j}{m}\leq\alpha\leq 1.

Then :

\left\|\nabla^{j}u\right\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\left\|\nabla^{m}u\right\|_{L^{r}}^{\alpha}\left\|u\right\|_{L^{q}}^{1-\alpha},

with

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{j}{2}+\left(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{m}{2}\right)\alpha+\frac{1-\alpha}{q}.

Appendix C
Third order energy estimate

In this section, we prove Proposition
7.1
. We split the proof into two lemmas : their goal is to point out the dependence of
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}
on non-linear terms in
\partial\nabla^{2}U
.

Lemma C.1
.

The energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\left(-\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega-e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

Proof.

We split
\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}
into two parts
A^{\varphi}+B^{\varphi}
:

\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{2}{N^{2}}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x}_{A^{\varphi}\vcentcolon=}+\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}\left|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x}_{B^{\varphi}\vcentcolon=}.

We start with
A^{\varphi}
, by writing
\partial_{t}=e_{0}+\beta\cdot\nabla
. Note that if for some function
f
we have
\left\|\nabla gf\right\|_{L^{1}}=O(\mathscr{R})
, then by integration by parts we have :

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}f\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e_{0}f\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\beta\cdot\nabla f\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e_{0}f\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\beta)f\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e_{0}f\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}).

Therefore, in what follows, we can forget about the
\beta\cdot\nabla
-part in
\partial_{t}
, which only contributes to
O(\mathscr{R})
. We now compute :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\left(\mathbf{T}^{2}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}^{2}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We then replace terms involving
\mathbf{T}^{2}
according to (
7.5
), and then replace
\Box_{g}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi
according to (
7.6
) (
F^{\varphi}_{ij}
and
\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\Box_{g}\omega
only contributes to
O(\mathscr{R})
) :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\left[-\Box_{g}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\Box_{g}\omega+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{2N}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\omega)\right]\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\left[-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi)+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{2N}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\omega)\right]\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We integrate by parts the terms with a divergence and expand :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\left(-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\left(-\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla(\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}(\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega)\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-8\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla(\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\omega)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\left(-e^{-4\varphi}\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

We now deal with
B^{\varphi}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-2\gamma}\left(\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\right)\cdot e_{0}\left(\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We see that the terms which contains
\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi
in
A^{\varphi}
and
B^{\varphi}
cancel each other, and that every terms wich are linear in
\partial\nabla^{2}U
only contribute to
O(\mathscr{R}(t))
, so that :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\left(-\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\omega-e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

∎

Lemma C.2
.

The energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega-e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\left(e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi-e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is very similar to the one of the previous lemma, except that we also differenciate the coefficient
e^{-4\varphi}
in the energy
\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}
. We split
\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}
into two parts
A^{\omega}+B^{\omega}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2N^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x}_{A^{\omega}\vcentcolon=}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\left|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x}_{B^{\omega}\vcentcolon=}.

We start by
A^{\omega}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\left(\mathbf{T}^{2}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}^{2}\varphi\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-2\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}^{2}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\varphi\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\left[-\Box_{g}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega+\frac{e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega)+2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\Box_{g}\varphi\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{2e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\varphi)+2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\Box_{g}\omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-\frac{2e^{-2\gamma}}{N}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\omega)-2\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\varphi-2\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega\right]\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\varphi\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We integrate by parts the terms with a divergence (note that we differenciate the
e^{-4\varphi}
, but the one with
\mathrm{div}(N\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega)
in front is the only divergence term which gives a main term) :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\varphi+\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-2e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi-2e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}4e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla\left(\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\varphi\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We now expand all the terms and note again that the linear terms in
\partial\nabla^{2}U
only contribute to
O(\mathscr{R}(t))
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}A^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}\left(e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega e_{0}\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi e_{0}\omega\right)\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathbf{T}\varphi+\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-2e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi-2e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\varphi|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega|^{2}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}6e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}(\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega)e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}(\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi)e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\varphi\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi-e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\varphi|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega|^{2}\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}(-\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega+2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi+2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega)\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

We now deal with
B^{\omega}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}B^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}\left(\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right)\cdot e_{0}\left(\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\varphi\left|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}(\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\varphi-2\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega)\cdot\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\varphi|\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega|^{2}\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

We see that the terms which contains
\nabla e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega
in
A^{\omega}
and
B^{\omega}
cancel each other, therefore :

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega+e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi-e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\varphi\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e^{-2\gamma}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\nabla\omega\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega-e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\left(e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi-e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-4\varphi}e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{T}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\mathbf{T}\omega-e^{-2\gamma}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\cdot\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}2e^{-2\gamma}e^{-4\varphi}\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\omega\cdot\left(e_{0}\omega\nabla\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi-e_{0}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi\nabla\omega\right)\mathrm{d}x+O(\mathscr{R}(t)).

Adding the two previous lemmas, we see that the main parts of
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\varphi}
and
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathscr{E}_{3}^{\omega}
cancel each other, and we obtain Proposition
7.1
.

∎
